|\,1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))|\leq C\,e^{-|s|/C}\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(3.19)

so that

\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}\Big(\,1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big)\,ds\Big|\leq C\,\int_{-\infty}^{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}\,e^{-|s|/C}\,ds\leq C\,e^{-R_{0}/C\,\varepsilon}\,,

and, recalling that
\omega_{n}\,R_{0}^{n}=1
,

|\kappa_{\varepsilon}|\leq C\,\varepsilon\,e^{-R_{0}/C\,\varepsilon}+C\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big|\,1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big|\,|s|^{j}\,ds\leq C\,\varepsilon^{2}\,,

where in the last inequality we have used (
3.19
) again. Taking into account the second formula for
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
in (
3.18
), we have thus proved

C\,\varepsilon^{2}\geq\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(w_{\varepsilon})-V(z_{0})\Big|\,.

(3.20)

With the same change of variables used before we have

C\,\varepsilon\geq\Big|\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big\{V\big(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\Big|\,,

while the decay properties of
f_{\varepsilon}
assumed in (
3.16
) give

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big\{V\big(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-V(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon}))\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,\int_{0}^{1}V^{\prime}\big(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon})+r\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)\,dr\,\leq C\,\varepsilon\,;

by combining the last two inequalities we thus find

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big\{V(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon}))-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\Big|

(3.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}|V(\eta(s-t_{\varepsilon}))-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))|\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,,

where in the last step we have used that
\tau\to V(\eta(.-\tau))
is strictly increasing in
\tau
. Since (
3.21
) implies
t_{\varepsilon}\to\tau_{0}
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, we can choose
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,W)
so that
|t_{\varepsilon}-\tau_{0}|\leq 1
and
R_{0}+\varepsilon\,(\tau_{0}-1)\geq R_{0}/2
. Since
V\circ\eta
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
, we have
|(V\circ\eta)^{\prime}|\geq 1/C
on
[-2,2]
, and noticing that if
|s-\tau_{0}|\leq 1
, then
|s-t_{\varepsilon}|<2
, we finally conclude

C\,\varepsilon\geq\int_{\tau_{0}-1}^{\tau_{0}+1}\frac{|(s-t_{\varepsilon})-(s-\tau_{0})|}{C}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\geq\frac{|\tau_{0}-t_{\varepsilon}|}{C}\,,

thus proving (
3.17
).

Step two
: We compute
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})
. Passing to the radial coordinate
r=|x|
, setting first
r=R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s
and then
t=s-\tau_{\varepsilon}
, recalling that
\eta^{\prime}=-\sqrt{W(\eta)}
, and exploiting the decay property (
A.16
) of
\eta
at
-\infty
, we find that, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\,\omega_{n}\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big(\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})^{2}+W(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon}))\Big)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

(3.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,n\,\omega_{n}\,\int_{-\tau_{\varepsilon}-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}W(\eta(t))\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon(t+\tau_{\varepsilon}))^{n-1}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,n\,\omega_{n}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W(\eta(t))\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon(t+\tau_{\varepsilon}))^{n-1}\,dt+{\rm O}(e^{-C/\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,n\,\omega_{n}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W(\eta(t))\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon(t+\tau_{0}))^{n-1}\,dt+{\rm O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,

where in the last step we have used
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\tau_{0}+{\rm O}(\varepsilon)
. Recalling that, by (
1.12
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(\eta)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{W(\eta)}\,\eta^{\prime}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi^{\prime}(\eta)\,\eta^{\prime}=\Phi(\eta(-\infty))-\Phi(\eta(+\infty))=\Phi(1)=1\,,

as well as that
\omega_{n}\,R_{0}^{n}=1
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})=2\,n\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+2\,n\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{2/n}\,(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1})\,\varepsilon+{\rm O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,

as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, that is (
3.3
). This proves the first part of the statement of the theorem.

Step three
: In preparation to the proof of the second part of the statement, we show that if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
satisfies

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq 2\,n\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+\ell_{0}\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1\,,

(3.23)

then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Phi(u)-1_{B_{R_{0}}}|^{n/(n-1)}\leq C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)\,.

(3.24)

Moreover, if
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, then
\sqrt{\ell_{0}}
can be replaced by
\ell_{0}
in (
3.24
).

Indeed, by (
3.23
), as seen in step one of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Phi(u)-(\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,r(u))^{1-n}\,1_{B_{r(u)}}|^{n/(n-1)}\leq C\,\sqrt{\ell_{0}}\,,

(3.25)

for some
r(u)\in(0,M_{0}]
, where
M_{0}
is a universal constant. Setting
f(r)=(\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,r)^{1-n}
, and noticing that
f(R_{0})=1
, it is enough to prove that

|r(u)-R_{0}|\leq C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)\,,\qquad\big|f(r(u))-1\big|\leq C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)\,.

(3.26)

Since
{\rm Lip}(f,[R_{0}/2,2R_{0}])\leq C
and
f(R_{0})=1
, it is enough to prove the first estimate in (
3.26
). To this end, we start noticing that if
r(u)<R_{0}
, then
f(r(u))>f(R_{0})=1\geq\Phi(u)
, and (
3.25
) gives

\displaystyle C\,\sqrt{\ell_{0}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r(u)}}|\Phi(u)-f(r(u))|^{n/(n-1)}\geq\omega_{n}\,r(u)^{n}\,\big(f(r(u))-1\big)^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\,r(u)^{n}\,\big(f(r(u))-f(R_{0})\big)^{n/(n-1)}=c(n)\,\Big(1-\big(r(u)/R_{0})^{n-1}\Big)^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c(n)\,(R_{0}-r(u))^{n/(n-1)}\,,

as desired. If, instead
r(u)>R_{0}
, then by
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)\leq\varepsilon\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C
,
f(r(u))\in(0,1)
and (
A.8
) (that is,
\Phi(b)-\Phi(a)\geq(b-a)^{2}/C
if
0\leq a\leq b\leq 1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{0}}}W(u)\geq\int_{B_{R_{0}}}W\Big(\Phi^{-1}\big(f(r(u))\big)\Big)-C\,\int_{B_{R_{0}}}\big|u-\Phi^{-1}(f(r(u)))\big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{0}}}W\Big(\Phi^{-1}\big(f(r(u))\big)\Big)-C\,\int_{B_{R_{0}}}\big|\Phi(u)-f(r(u))\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{0}}}W\Big(\Phi^{-1}\big(f(r(u))\big)\Big)-C\,\int_{B_{R_{0}}}\big|\Phi(u)-f(r(u))\big|^{n/(n-1)}\,,

where in the last inequality we have just used
2\geq n/(n-1)
and the fact that
\Phi(u)
and
f(r(u))
lie in
[0,1]
. Hence, by
B_{R_{0}}\subset B_{r(u)}
, (
3.25
) and
\omega_{n}\,R_{0}^{n}=1
,

W\big(\Phi^{-1}(f(r(u)))\big)\leq C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)\,.

Now,
R_{0}<r(u)\leq M_{0}
implies
1>f(r(u))\geq f(M_{0})\geq\delta_{0}
(provided we further decrease the value of
\delta_{0}
). In particular, by
W(t)\geq(1-t)^{2}/C
on
(\delta_{0},1)
(which can be assumed as done with (
A.13
)), we have

C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)\geq\Big(1-\Phi^{-1}\big(f(r(u))\big)\Big)^{2}\,.

By (
A.7
), we have

1-\Phi^{-1}(s)\geq\frac{\sqrt{1-s}}{C}\,,\qquad\forall s\in(0,1)\,,

thus concluding

\displaystyle C\,\big((\sqrt{\ell_{0}})^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-f(r(u))=c(n)\,\big(R_{0}^{1-n}-r(u)^{1-n}\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c(n)}{r(u)^{n-1}}\,\Big(\Big(\frac{r(u)}{R_{0}}\Big)^{n-1}-1\Big)\geq\frac{c(n)}{M_{0}^{n-1}}\,(r(u)-R_{0})\,.

This completes the proof of (
3.26
), and thus of (
3.24
).

Step four
: We now consider
\{\varepsilon_{j},v_{j},\mathrm{E}_{j}\}_{j}
as in the statement, and begin the proof of the resolution result. We introduce the radius
R_{j}(t)
by setting
v_{j}(R_{j}(t))=t
for every
t
in the range of
v_{j}
. In this step we prove that both
\delta_{0}
(defined in section
A.3
) and
1-\delta_{0}
belong to the range of each
v_{j}
, that

\displaystyle 3\,R_{0}\geq R_{j}(\delta_{0})\geq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})\geq\frac{R_{0}}{3}\,,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{C}\leq R_{j}(\delta_{0})-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(3.28)

and that

-\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{j}}\leq v_{j}^{\prime}\leq-\frac{1}{C\varepsilon_{j}}\qquad\mbox{on $(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}),R_{j}(\delta_{0}))$}\,.

(3.29)

In particular, the constants
b_{j}
and
c_{j}
introduced in (
3.10
) are well-defined, they satisfy

c_{j}=R_{j}(\delta_{0})-R_{0}\,,\qquad b_{j}=R_{0}-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})\,,

(3.30)

and property (
3.12
) in the statement boils down to (
3.29
).

By step three, for
j
large enough and considering that
v_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|1_{B_{R_{0}}}-\Phi(v_{j})|^{n/(n-1)}\leq C\,\big(\ell_{0}^{(n-1)/n}+\varepsilon_{0}\big)\,.

(3.31)

By (
3.31
), if
\ell_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
are small enough, then both
\delta_{0}
and
1-\delta_{0}
must belong to the range of each
v_{j}
. Now, if
R_{j}(\delta_{0})\leq R_{0}
, then

\int_{B_{R_{0}}\setminus B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}}|1_{B_{R_{0}}}-\Phi(v_{j})|^{n/(n-1)}\geq\omega_{n}\,(R_{0}^{n}-R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{n})\,(1-\Phi(\delta_{0}))^{n/(n-1)}\geq\frac{R_{0}^{n}-R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{n}}{C}\,,

and
R_{j}(\delta_{0})\geq R_{0}/2
follows by (
3.31
) for
\ell_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough; if, instead,
R_{j}(\delta_{0})\geq R_{0}
, then

\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}\setminus B_{R_{0}}}|1_{B_{R_{0}}}-\Phi(v_{j})|^{n/(n-1)}\geq\omega_{n}\,(R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{n}-R_{0}^{n})\,\Phi(\delta_{0})^{n/(n-1)}\geq\frac{R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{n}-R_{0}^{n}}{C}\,,

and
R_{j}(\delta_{0})\leq 2\,R_{0}
follows, again, for
\ell_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough; we have thus proved
R_{0}/2\leq R_{j}(\delta_{0})\leq 2\,R_{0}
. Since (
3.5
) implies
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})\leq C
we also have

C\,\varepsilon_{j}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(v_{j})\geq\frac{R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{n}-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})^{n}}{C}\geq\frac{R_{j}(\delta_{0})-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}{C}

where in the last inequality we have used
R_{j}(\delta_{0})\geq R/2
. Thus, we have so far proved (
3.27
) and the upper bound in (
3.28
). Before proving the lower bound in (
3.28
), we prove (
3.29
). To this end, we multiply (
3.6
) by
v_{j}^{\prime}
, and then integrate over an arbitrary interval
(0,r)
, to get

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big((v_{j}^{\prime})^{2}+2\,(n-1)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{v_{j}^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{t}\,dt\Big)=W(v_{j})-W(v_{j}(0))-\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{0}^{r}v_{j}\,(1-v_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}\,v_{j}^{\prime}\,.

(3.32)

By (
3.7
), the right-hand side of (
3.32
) is bounded in terms of
n
and
W
, so that (
3.32
) implies
\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,(v_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\leq C
on
(0,\infty)
; the lower bound in (
3.29
) then follows by
v_{j}^{\prime}\leq 0
. To obtain the upper bound in (
3.29
), we multiply again (
3.6
) by
v_{j}^{\prime}
, but this time we integrate over
(r,\infty)
for
r\in(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}),R_{j}(\delta_{0}))
, thus obtaining

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big(-v_{j}^{\prime}(r)^{2}+2\,(n-1)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{v_{j}^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{t}\,dt\Big)=-W(v_{j}(r))-\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{r}^{\infty}v_{j}\,(1-v_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}\,v_{j}^{\prime}\,.

(3.33)

By
W(v_{j}(r))\geq\inf_{[\delta_{0},1-\delta_{0}]}W\geq 1/C
, (
3.7
), and the non-negativity of the integral on the left-hand side of (
3.33
), we deduce that

2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,v_{j}^{\prime}(r)^{2}\geq W(v_{j}(r))-C\,\varepsilon_{j}\geq\frac{1}{C}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}),R_{j}(\delta_{0}))\,,

which, again by
v_{j}^{\prime}\leq 0
, implies the upper bound in (
3.29
). To finally prove the lower bound in (
3.28
), we notice that thanks to the lower bound in (
3.29
) we have

\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,(R_{j}(\delta_{0})-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))\geq\int_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}^{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}(-v_{j}^{\prime})=1-2\,\delta_{0}\,.

We have completed the proofs of (
3.27
), (
3.28
) and (
3.29
).

Step five
: We obtain sharp estimates for
v_{j}
as
r\to\infty
: precisely, we prove that for every
r\geq R_{j}(\delta_{0})
one has

\displaystyle v_{j}(r)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,e^{-{(r-R_{j}(\delta_{0}))/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}}\,,

(3.34)

\displaystyle|v_{j}^{(k)}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{j}^{k}}\,\,e^{-(r-R_{j}(\delta_{0}))/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,\qquad k=1,2\,.

(3.35)

By (
A.6
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) we have that

2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big\{v_{j}^{\prime\prime}+(n-1)\,\frac{v_{j}^{\prime}}{r}\Big\}\geq\frac{v_{j}}{C}-C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,v_{j}\geq\frac{v_{j}}{C}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(R_{j}(\delta_{0}),\infty)$}\,.

(3.36)

Multiplying by
v_{j}^{\prime}
and integrating over
(r,\infty)\subset(R_{j}(\delta_{0}),\infty)
we obtain

-\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,v_{j}^{\prime}(r)^{2}\geq-\frac{v_{j}(r)^{2}}{C}\,,\qquad\forall r\geq R_{j}(\delta_{0})\,.

(3.37)

Plugging (
3.37
) into (
3.36
) we reabsorb the
v_{j}^{\prime}/r
term and obtain

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,v_{j}^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{v_{j}}{C_{*}}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(R_{j}(\delta_{0}),\infty)$}\,,

(3.38)

for a universal constant
C_{*}
. We now notice that

v_{*}(r)=\delta_{0}\,e^{-(r-R_{j}(\delta_{0}))/\sqrt{C_{*}}\,{\varepsilon_{j}}}\,,

satisfies
\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,v_{*}^{\prime\prime}=v_{*}/C_{*}
and

v_{*}(R_{j}(\delta_{0}))=\delta_{0}=v_{j}(R_{j}(\delta_{0}))\,.

Therefore
v_{j}\leq v_{*}
on
(R_{j}(\delta_{0}),\infty)
, and (
3.34
) follows. The case
k=1
of (
3.35
) follows from (
3.34
) and (
3.37
). The case
k=2
of (
3.35
) follows by noticing that (
3.6
), (
3.7
), (
3.37
) and (
A.6
) imply

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,|v_{j}^{\prime\prime}|\leq C\,\Big(\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,|v_{j}^{\prime}|+|W^{\prime}(v_{j})|+\varepsilon_{j}\,v_{j}\Big)\leq C\,\Big(\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,|v_{j}^{\prime}|+v_{j}\Big)\leq C\,v_{j}\,,

and then by using (
3.34
) again.

Step six
: We obtain sharp estimates for
v_{j}(r)
when
r\to 0^{+}
: precisely, we prove that for every
r\leq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})
one has

\displaystyle 1-v_{j}(r)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\,e^{-{(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}}\,,

(3.39)

\displaystyle|v_{j}^{\prime}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\min\Big\{\frac{r}{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}},\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big\}\,\,e^{-(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,

(3.40)

\displaystyle|v_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\,\,e^{-(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,.

(3.41)

To this end, it is convenient to recast (
3.6
) in terms of
w_{j}=1-v_{j}
, so that

2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big\{w_{j}^{\prime\prime}+(n-1)\,\frac{w_{j}^{\prime}}{r}\Big\}=-W^{\prime}(1-w_{j})+\varepsilon_{j}\,\,w_{j}\,(1-w_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}\,.

(3.42)

By (
A.6
) and (
3.7
), if
r\leq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})
, then

-W^{\prime}(1-w_{j})+\varepsilon_{j}\,\,w_{j}\,(1-w_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}\leq C\,(1-w_{j})

(3.43)

so that (
3.42
) implies in particular

2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big\{w_{j}^{\prime\prime}+(n-1)\,\frac{w_{j}^{\prime}}{r}\Big\}\leq C\,w_{j}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))$}\,.

(3.44)

Multiplying by
w_{j}^{\prime}\geq 0
and integrating on
(0,r)\subset(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))
we deduce

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\Big\{w_{j}^{\prime}(r)^{2}+\int_{0}^{r}\frac{(w_{j}^{\prime})^{2}}{t}\Big\}\leq C\,(w_{j}(r)^{2}-w_{j}(0)^{2})\leq C\,w_{j}(r)^{2}\,,

that is,

\varepsilon_{j}\,w_{j}^{\prime}\leq C\,w_{j}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))$}\,.

(3.45)

Combining (
3.45
) with (
3.42
), (
A.6
) and (
3.7
), we find that

\displaystyle 2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,w_{j}^{\prime\prime}+C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,w_{j}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Big\{w_{j}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{n-1}{r}\,w_{j}^{\prime}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-W^{\prime}(1-w_{j})+\varepsilon_{j}\,\,w_{j}\,(1-w_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}\geq\frac{w_{j}}{C}\,-C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,w_{j}\,,

on
[R_{0}/4,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))
, so that, for
j
large enough and for a constant
C_{*}
depending on
n
and
W
only, we have

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,w_{j}^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{w_{j}}{C_{*}}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $[R_{0}/4,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))$}\,.

(3.46)

Correspondingly to
C_{*}
, we introduce the barrier

w_{*}(r)=\delta_{0}\,\Big\{e^{((R_{0}/4)-r)/\sqrt{C_{*}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}}+e^{(r-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))/\sqrt{C_{*}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}}\Big\}\,,\qquad r>0\,.

By the monotonicity of
w_{j}
and by
R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})\geq R_{0}/3
(recall (
3.27
)),

\displaystyle w_{*}(R_{0}/4)\geq\delta_{0}=w_{j}(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))\geq w_{j}(R_{0}/4)\,,

\displaystyle w_{*}(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))\geq\delta_{0}=w_{j}(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,w_{*}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{w_{*}}{C_{*}}\qquad\mbox{on $[0,\infty)$}\,.

We thus find
w_{j}\leq w_{*}
on
[R_{0}/4,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))
, that is, for every
R_{0}/4\leq r\leq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})
,

1-v_{j}(r)\leq\delta_{0}\,\Big\{e^{((R_{0}/4)-r)/\sqrt{C_{*}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}}+e^{(r-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))/\sqrt{C_{*}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}}\Big\}\,.

(3.47)

By testing (
3.47
) with

r_{*}=\frac{R_{0}/4+R_{0}/3}{2}

and exploiting the monotonicity of
v_{j}
, we find that for
r\in(0,r_{*}]

1-v_{j}(r)\leq\delta_{0}\,e^{-1/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\qquad\forall r\in(0,r_{*}]\,,

(3.48)

(thus obtaining the crucial information that, for
j
large enough and for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|1-v_{j}\|_{C^{0}[0,r_{*}]}={\rm o}(\varepsilon_{j}^{k})
as
j\to\infty
). At the same time, for
r_{*}\leq r\leq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})
, the second exponential in (
3.47
) is bounded from below in terms of a universal constant, while the first exponential is bounded from above by
e^{-1/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}
, so that (
3.47
) and (
3.48
) can be combined into

1-v_{j}(r)\leq C\,e^{-(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})]\,,

that is (
3.39
). By combining (
3.39
) and (
3.45
) we also find

-v_{j}^{\prime}(r)\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,e^{-(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})]\,,

(3.49)

which is half of the estimate for
|v_{j}^{\prime}|
in (
3.40
). Multiplying (
3.44
) by
r^{n-1}
we find

2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\big(r^{n-1}\,w_{j}^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}\leq C\,r^{n-1}\,w_{j}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})]\,,

which we integrate over
(0,r)\subset(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))
to conclude that

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,r^{n-1}\,(-v_{j}^{\prime}(r))\leq C\,\int_{0}^{r}w_{j}(t)\,t^{n-1}dt\leq C\,(1-v_{j}(r))\,r^{n}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})]\,;

in particular, by combining this last inequality with (
3.39
) we find

-v_{j}^{\prime}(r)\leq C\,\frac{r}{\varepsilon_{j}^{2}}\,e^{-(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})]\,,

that is the missing half of (
3.40
). Finally, by (
3.42
) with (
3.43
) we find

\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,|v_{j}^{\prime\prime}|\leq C\,\Big\{(1-v_{j})+\frac{|v_{j}^{\prime}|}{r}\Big\}\qquad\mbox{on $(0,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))$}\,,

and then (
3.41
) follows from (
3.39
) and (
3.40
).

Step seven
: We now improve the first set of inequalities in (
3.27
), and show that

R_{0}-C\,\varepsilon_{j}\leq R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})<R_{j}(\delta_{0})\leq R_{0}+C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,.

(3.50)

Let us set

\alpha_{j}=\int_{B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}V(v_{j})\,,\qquad\beta_{j}=\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}\setminus B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}V(v_{j})\,,\qquad\gamma_{j}=\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{c}}}V(v_{j})\,.

By (
A.11
), (
3.39
) and (
3.27
) we have

\displaystyle\big|\alpha_{j}-\omega_{n}\,R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})^{n}\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}1-V(v_{j})\leq C\,\int_{B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}(1-v_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\int_{B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}e^{-{(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-|x|)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\,\int_{0}^{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}\,e^{-{(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})-r)/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}}\,r^{n-1}\,dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})/\varepsilon_{j}}^{0}\,e^{s/C}\,(R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,.

Similarly, by (
A.11
), (
3.27
) and (
3.34
) we find

\displaystyle|\gamma_{j}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}^{c}}V(v_{j})\leq C\,\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})^{c}}}v_{j}^{2\,n/(n-1)}\leq C\,\int_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}^{\infty}\,e^{-{(r-R_{j}(\delta_{0}))/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}}\,r^{n-1}\,dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,e^{-s/C}\,(R_{j}(\delta_{0})+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,.

Finally, thanks to (
3.27
),

\displaystyle|\beta_{j}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{j}(\delta_{0})}\setminus B_{R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})}}V(v_{j})\leq C\,(R_{j}(\delta_{0})-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0}))\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,.

Combining the estimates for
\alpha_{j}
,
\beta_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}
with the fact that

\omega_{n}\,R_{0}^{n}=1=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(v_{j})=\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}+\gamma_{j}\,,

we conclude that

C\,\varepsilon_{j}\geq\omega_{n}\,|R_{0}^{n}-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})^{n}|\leq\frac{|R_{0}-R_{j}(1-\delta_{0})|}{C}\,,

so that (
3.50
) follows by (
3.27
).

Step eight
: We conclude the proof of the theorem: (
3.29
), (
3.30
) and (
3.50
) imply (
3.10
) and (
3.12
), as well as

|b_{j}|\,,|c_{j}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(3.51)

which is a weaker form of (
3.11
); (
3.34
) and (
3.35
) imply (
3.13
), while (
3.39
), (
3.40
), and (
3.41
) imply (
3.14
). We are thus left to prove the full form of (
3.11
) (which includes a positive lower bound in the from
\varepsilon_{j}/C
for both
b_{j}
and
c_{j}
), as well as (
3.8
): that is, we want to show that if
v_{j}
satisfies (
3.4
), (
3.5
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), then, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
j
large enough, we have

v_{j}(x)=z_{\varepsilon_{j}}(x)+f_{j}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)=\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}-\tau_{j}\Big)+f_{j}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)\,,

(3.52)

with functions
f_{j}\in C^{2}(I_{j})
such that

|f_{j}(s)|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/C}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}=(-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j},\infty)\,,

(3.53)

and with
\tau_{j}=\tau_{\varepsilon_{j}}
for
\tau_{\varepsilon}
defined by (
3.1
) and (
3.2
). In fact, (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) imply the full form of (
3.11
): for example, combined with (
3.12
) and (
3.17
), they give

\displaystyle C\,\frac{b_{j}}{\varepsilon_{j}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{R_{0}-b_{j}}^{R_{0}}(-v_{j}^{\prime})=v_{j}(R_{0}-b_{j})-v_{j}(R_{0})=(1-\delta_{0})-\eta(-\tau_{j})-f_{j}(0)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-\delta_{0}-\eta(-\tau_{0})-C\,\varepsilon_{j}

where the latter quantity is positive provided
j
is large enough and we further decrease the value of
\delta_{0}
to have
\delta_{0}<1-\eta(-\tau_{0})
.

We can thus focus on (
3.52
) and (
3.53
), which we recast by looking at the functions

\eta_{j}(s)=v_{j}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)\,,\qquad s\in I_{j}\,,

in terms of which
f_{j}(s)=\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-\tau_{j})
. Thus, our goal becomes proving that

|\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-\tau_{j})|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/C}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.54)

We start noticing that, by (
3.12
), (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), we have

\displaystyle C\geq-\eta_{j}^{\prime}(s)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,,\hskip 76.82234pt\qquad\forall s\in(-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j},c_{j}/\varepsilon_{j})\,,

(3.55)

\displaystyle\eta_{j}^{(k)}(s)\leq C\,e^{-s/C}\,,\hskip 76.82234pt\qquad\forall s\in(c_{j}/\varepsilon_{j},\infty)\,,k=0,1,2,

(3.56)

\left\{\begin{split}&(1-\eta_{j}(s))+|\eta^{\prime\prime}_{j}(s)|\leq C\,e^{s/C}\,,\\
&|\eta_{j}^{\prime}|\leq C\,\min\Big\{\frac{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,,1\Big\}\,e^{s/C}\,,\end{split}\right.\qquad\forall s\in(-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j},-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j})\,,

(3.57)

(while the analogous estimates for
\eta
are found in (
A.16
) and (
A.18
)). In order to estimate
f_{j}(s)=\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-\tau_{j})
, we introduce

g_{j}(s)=\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-t_{j})\,,

for
t_{j}
defined by the identity

\eta\big(-(b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j})-t_{j}\big)=1-\delta_{0}\,.

(3.58)

(Notice that the definition is well-posed by
\eta^{\prime}<0
and
\eta(\mathbb{R})=(0,1)
.) We claim that the proof of (
3.53
) can be reduced to that of

|g_{j}(s)|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/C}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.59)

Indeed, by (
3.4
), if (
3.59
) holds, then we are in the position to apply step one, and deduce from (
3.17
) that
|t_{j}-\tau_{0}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}
. Having also (by the same argument)
|\tau_{j}-\tau_{0}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}
, we deduce that

|\tau_{j}-t_{j}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,

that we exploit in combination with (
3.56
) and (
3.57
) to deduce

\displaystyle|f_{j}(s)-g_{j}(s)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta(s-t_{j})-\eta(s-\tau_{j})|\leq C\,\int_{0}^{1}\big|\eta^{\prime}\big(s-\tau_{j}-t\,(t_{j}-\tau_{j})\big)\big|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/C}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

We are thus left to prove (
3.59
). To this end, we preliminarily notice that, since
\eta_{j}(-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j})=v_{j}(R_{0}-b_{j})=1-\delta_{0}
, the definition of
t_{j}
is such that

g_{j}\big(-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j}\big)=0\,.

(3.60)

Moreover, by the decay properties (
A.16
) of
\eta
and by
|b_{j}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}
, (
3.58
) implies

|t_{j}|\leq C\,.

(3.61)

We now divide the proof of (
3.59
) in three separate arguments:

We prove (
3.59
) for
|s|\geq C\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})
: This is trivial from the decay properties of
\eta
and
\eta_{j}
. Indeed, by (
A.16
), (
3.61
), (
3.56
) and (
3.57
) we find that

|g_{j}(s)|\leq K_{1}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.62)

for a universal constant
K_{1}
. In particular, we trivially have

|g_{j}(s)|\leq K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/2\,K_{1}}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,,|s|\geq 2\,K_{1}\log\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)\,.

(3.63)

We will later increase the value of
K_{1}
in (
3.62
) so that (
3.74
) below holds too.

We prove (
3.59
) on arbitrary compact subsets of
I_{j}
: More precisely, we show that for every
K>0
we can find
C_{K}=C_{K}(n,W)
(that is, a constant that depends on
n
,
W
and
K
only) such that

|g_{j}(s)|\leq C_{K}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,,|s|\leq K\,.

(3.64)

To this end, setting
\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}(s)=\mathrm{E}_{j}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)
, we deduce from (
3.6
) that
\eta_{j}
satisfies the ODE

2\,\eta_{j}^{\prime\prime}+2\,\varepsilon_{j}\,\frac{n-1}{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s}\,\eta_{j}^{\prime}=W^{\prime}(\eta_{j})-\varepsilon_{j}\,\eta_{j}\,(1-\eta_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}\qquad\mbox{on $I_{j}$}\,.

(3.65)

Multiplying (
3.65
) by
-\eta_{j}^{\prime}
and integrating over
(s,\infty)
we find

\eta_{j}^{\prime}(s)^{2}-2\,\varepsilon_{j}\,(n-1)\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{\eta_{j}^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,t}\,dt=W(\eta_{j}(s))+\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\eta_{j}\,(1-\eta_{j})\,\eta_{j}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}\,.

(3.66)

Since
\eta^{\prime}(s-t_{j})^{2}=W(\eta(s-t_{j}))
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we find that

\displaystyle\eta_{j}^{\prime}(s)^{2}-\eta^{\prime}(s-t_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W(\eta_{j}(s))-W(\eta(s-t_{j}))+\varepsilon_{j}\,L_{j}(s)\,,

(3.67)

where

\displaystyle L_{j}(s)=\int_{s}^{\infty}\Big(2\,(n-1)\,\frac{\eta_{j}^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,t}+\eta_{j}\,(1-\eta_{j})\,\eta_{j}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}\Big)\,dt\,.

Setting

\ell_{j}(s)=\frac{W(\eta_{j}(s))-W(\eta(s-t_{j}))}{\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-t_{j})}\,,\qquad d_{j}(s)=\eta_{j}^{\prime}(s)+\eta^{\prime}(s-t_{j})\,,\qquad\Gamma_{j}(s)=\frac{\ell_{j}(s)}{d_{j}(s)}\,,

and noticing that
d_{j}<0
on
I_{j}
, (
3.67
) takes the form

\displaystyle g_{j}^{\prime}(s)-\Gamma_{j}(s)\,g_{j}(s)=\frac{\varepsilon_{j}\,L_{j}(s)}{d_{j}(s)}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.68)

Multiplying (
3.68
) by
\exp(-\int_{0}^{s}\Gamma_{j})
, integrating over an interval
(-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j},s)
, and taking into account (
3.60
), we find

\displaystyle g_{j}(s)\,e^{-\int_{0}^{s}\Gamma_{j}}=\varepsilon_{j}\int_{-b_{j}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{s}\,\frac{e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma_{j}}}{d_{j}(t)}\,L_{j}(t)\,dt\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.69)

We now notice that by
(
3.7
), (
3.56
) and (
3.57
),

|L_{j}(s)|\leq C\,\min\{1,e^{-s/C}\}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,.

(3.70)

Moreover, by
{\rm Lip}\,W\leq C
we have
|\ell_{j}|\leq C
on
I_{j}
, while
\eta_{j}^{\prime}\leq 0
and (
3.61
) give

d_{j}(s)\leq\eta^{\prime}(s-t_{j})\leq-\frac{1}{C_{K}}\,,\qquad\forall|s|\leq K\,,

(3.71)

and, in particular,
|\Gamma_{j}(s)|\leq C_{K}
for
|s|\leq K
. Now, assuming without loss of generality that
K
is large enough to entail
K\geq|b_{j}|/\varepsilon_{j}
(as we can do since
|b_{j}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}
for a universal constant
C
), then (
3.69
), (
3.70
), (
3.71
) and
|\Gamma_{j}|\leq C_{K}
on
[-K,K]
combined give (
3.64
).

Finally, we prove (
3.59
) in the remaining case
: Having in mind (
3.63
) and (
3.64
) we are left to prove the existence of a sufficiently large universal constant
K_{2}
such that (
3.59
) holds (provided
j
is large enough) for every
s\in I_{j}
with
K_{2}\leq|s|\leq 2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})
. To this end, we start by subtracting
2\,\eta^{\prime\prime}=W(\eta)
from (
3.65
), and obtain

2g_{j}^{\prime\prime}-m_{j}\,g_{j}=\varepsilon_{j}\,\Big\{\eta_{j}\,(1-\eta_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}\,-2\,(n-1)\,\frac{\eta_{j}^{\prime}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s}\Big\}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,,

(3.72)

where

m_{j}(s)=\frac{W^{\prime}(\eta_{j}(s))-W^{\prime}(\eta(s-t_{j}))}{\eta_{j}(s)-\eta(s-t_{j})}\,,\qquad s\in I_{j}\,.

The coefficient
m_{j}
is uniformly positive: indeed, the decay properties of
\eta
and
\eta_{j}
at infinity, combined with
|t_{j}|\leq C
, imply the existence of a universal constant
K_{2}
such that if
|s|\geq K_{2}
, then
\eta_{j}(s)
and
\eta(s-t_{j})
are both at distance at most
\delta_{0}
from
\{0,1\}
: and since
W^{\prime\prime}\geq 1/C
on
(0,\delta_{0})\cup(1-\delta_{0},1)
by (
A.6
), we conclude that, up to further increase the value of
K_{2}
,

m_{j}(s)\geq\frac{1}{K_{2}}\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,,|s|\geq K_{2}\,.

(3.73)

At the same time, the right-hand side of (
3.72
) has exponential decay: indeed, by (
3.7
), (
3.55
), (
3.56
) and (
3.57
), if
|s|\leq\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})
,
s\in I_{j}
, then we get

\Big|\eta_{j}\,(1-\eta_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}^{*}\,-2\,(n-1)\,\frac{\eta_{j}^{\prime}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s}\Big|\leq K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}\,,

(3.74)

up to further increase the value of the universal constant
K_{1}
introduced in (
3.63
). Let us thus consider

g_{*}(s)=C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/\sqrt{2\,C_{2}}}\,,\qquad s\in\mathbb{R}\,,

for
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
universal constants to be determined. By combining (
3.72
) with (
3.73
) and (
3.74
) we find that, if
s\in I_{j}
with
K_{2}\leq|s|\leq 2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})
, then

\displaystyle 2\,(g_{j}-g_{*})^{\prime\prime}-m_{j}\,(g_{j}-g_{*})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle m_{j}\,g_{*}-2\,g_{*}^{\prime\prime}-K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{K_{2}}-\frac{1}{C_{2}}\Big)\,g_{*}-K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}\,\Big\{\frac{C_{1}}{K_{1}}\,\Big(\frac{1}{K_{2}}-\frac{1}{C_{2}}\Big)\,e^{[(1/K_{1})-(1/\sqrt{2\,C_{2}})]\,|s|}-1\Big\}\,K_{1}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}\,,

where the latter quantity is non-negative for every
|s|\geq K_{2}
provided

C_{1}\geq 3\,K_{1}\,K_{2}\,e^{-K_{0}/2\,K_{1}}\,,\qquad C_{2}\geq\max\{2\,K_{2},2\,K_{1}^{2}\}\,.

(3.75)

At the same time, by (
3.63
),

\big|g_{j}\big(\pm\,2\,K_{1}\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})\big)\big|\leq K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,,

while
C_{2}\geq 2\,K_{1}^{2}
gives

g_{*}\big(\pm 2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})\big)=C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})/\sqrt{2\,C_{2}}}\geq C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,.

Upon further requiring
C_{1}\geq K_{1}
we thus have

g_{*}(s)\geq|g_{j}(s)|\qquad\mbox{at $s=\pm\,2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})$}\,.

(3.76)

Similarly, by (
3.64
),

|g_{j}(\pm K_{2})|\leq C_{K_{2}}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,

while
C_{\geq}2\,K_{2}
gives

g_{*}(\pm K_{2})=C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-K_{2}/\sqrt{2\,C_{2}}}\geq C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-\sqrt{K_{2}}/2}\,.

Upon requiring that
C_{1}\geq C_{K_{2}}\,e^{\sqrt{K_{2}}/2}
, we find that

g_{*}(s)\geq|g_{j}(s)|\qquad\mbox{at $s=\pm\,K_{2}$}\,.

(3.77)

In summary, we have proved that if
K_{1}
satisfies (
3.62
) and (
3.74
),
K_{2}
satisfies (
3.73
), and
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are taken large enough in terms of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, then (
3.76
) and (
3.77
) holds. In particular,
h_{j}=g_{j}-g_{*}
is non-positive on the boundary of the intervals
[-2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j}),-K_{2}]
and
[K_{2},2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})]
, with
h_{j}^{\prime\prime}-m_{j}\,h\geq 0
,
m_{j}\geq 0
, on those intervals thanks to (
3.75
) and (
3.73
); correspondingly, by the maximum principle,
h_{j}\leq 0
there, that is,

g_{j}(s)\leq C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/\sqrt{2\,C_{2}}}\,,\qquad\forall s\in I_{j}\,,K_{2}\leq|s|\leq 2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})\,.

To get the matching lower bound we notice that, again by (
3.74
),

(-g_{*}-g_{j})^{\prime\prime}-m_{j}\,(-g_{*}-g_{j})\geq m_{j}\,g_{*}-g_{*}^{\prime\prime}-K_{1}\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/K_{1}}

so that, by the same considerations made before, the maximum principle can be applied to
k_{j}=-g_{*}-g_{j}
on
[-2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j}),-K_{2}]\cup[K_{2},2\,K_{1}\,\log(1/\varepsilon_{j})]
, to deduce
g_{j}\geq-g_{*}
. This completes the proof of (
3.59
).
∎

4.
Strict stability among radial functions

In this section we are going to exploit the resolution result in Theorem
3.1
to deduce a stability estimate for
\psi(\varepsilon)
on radial (not necessarily decreasing) functions. More precisely, we shall prove the following statement.

Theorem 4.1

(Flugede type estimate)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exist universal constants
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
with the following property: if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
is a minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
, and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
is radial and such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1\,,

(4.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(u-u_{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|u-u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\delta_{0}\,,

(4.3)

then, setting
h=u-u_{\varepsilon}
,

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)-\psi(\varepsilon)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,.

(4.4)

Before entering into the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we show how it can be used to improve on the conclusions of Theorem
2.1
. In particular, it gives the uniqueness of minimizers in
\psi(\varepsilon)
and, together with the resolution result in Theorem
3.1
, allows to compute the precise asymptotic behavior of
\psi(\varepsilon)
and
\lambda(\varepsilon)
up to second and first order in
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
respectively. Notice in particular that (
4.7
) sharply improves (
2.3
).

Corollary 4.2
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}
such that, if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then
\psi(\varepsilon)
admits a unique minimizer (modulo translations). In particular, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
\lambda(\varepsilon)
is unambiguously defined as the Lagrange multiplier of the unique minimizer
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
of
\psi(\varepsilon)
by the identity (
2.2
), i.e.

\lambda(\varepsilon)=\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\,\psi(\varepsilon)+\,\frac{1}{n}\,\Big\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\Big\}\,.

(4.5)

Finally,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})\mapsto\lambda(\varepsilon)
is continuous and the following expansions hold as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\psi(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,n\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+2\,n\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{2/n}\,\kappa_{0}\,\varepsilon+{\rm O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(4.6)

\displaystyle\lambda(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+{\rm O}(\varepsilon)\,,

(4.7)

where
\kappa_{0}=\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\eta^{\prime}\,V^{\prime}(\eta)+W(\eta)]\,s\,ds
.

Proof of Corollary
4.2
.

Step one
: Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and let
u_{\varepsilon}
and
v_{\varepsilon}
be two minimizers of
\psi(\varepsilon)
, so that, up to translations,
u_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
thanks to Theorem
2.1
. By Theorem
3.1
, if we set
h_{\varepsilon}=v_{\varepsilon}-u_{\varepsilon}
, then

h_{\varepsilon}(x)=f_{\varepsilon}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,,

where
f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{2}(-R_{0}/\varepsilon,\infty)
, and

|f_{\varepsilon}(s)|\leq C\,\varepsilon\,e^{-s/C}\,,\qquad\forall s\geq-R_{0}/\varepsilon\,.

(4.8)

We thus see that
u=v_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
4.1
) and (
4.3
). Moreover, by (
4.8
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h_{\varepsilon}^{2}=n\,\omega_{n}\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq C\,\varepsilon^{2}\,,

so that (
4.2
) holds too. We can thus apply (
4.4
) with
u=v_{\varepsilon}
, and exploit the minimality of
v_{\varepsilon}
to deduce that

0=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})-\psi(\varepsilon)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\frac{h_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,,

that is
h_{\varepsilon}=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as claimed.

Step two
: We prove (
4.6
) and (
4.7
). If
u_{\varepsilon}
is the minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, then by Theorem
3.1
we have
u_{\varepsilon}(x)=z_{\varepsilon}(x)+f_{\varepsilon}((|x|-R_{0})/\varepsilon)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and with
f_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
4.8
). Moreover, as proved in (
3.3
), we have

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})=2\,n\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+2\,n\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{2/n}\,\kappa_{0}+{\rm O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,.

Since
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})
, we are left to prove that
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\geq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})-C\,\varepsilon^{2}
. Setting
|x|=R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s
we have

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,,\qquad\nabla u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)}{\varepsilon}\,\frac{x}{|x|}\,,

while
z_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the same identities with
f_{\varepsilon}=0
, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})=\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\Big(2\,\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\,f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)^{2}\Big)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

(4.9)

\displaystyle+\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big(W\big(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-W(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon}))\Big)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,.

Integration by parts and
2\,\eta^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime}(\eta)
give

\displaystyle\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,2\,\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\,f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,W^{\prime}\big(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\big)\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle-2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-2}\,ds\,.

Dropping the non-negative term with
f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)^{2}
in (
4.9
), and noticing that, by (
A.5
) and (
4.8
), we have

\Big|W\big(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})+f_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-W(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon}))-W^{\prime}\big(\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\big)\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\Big|\leq C\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)^{2}\,,

for every
s>-R_{0}/\varepsilon
, we thus find

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\eta^{\prime}(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-2}\,ds

\displaystyle-C\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}f_{\varepsilon}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\geq-C\,\varepsilon^{2}\,,

where in the last inequality we have used (
4.8
),
|\tau_{\varepsilon}|\leq C
and the decay estimate for
\eta^{\prime}
in (
A.18
). Coming to (
4.7
), rearranging terms in (
4.5
) we have

\displaystyle\lambda(\varepsilon)=\Big(1-\frac{2}{n}\Big)\,\psi(\varepsilon)+\,\frac{2}{n}\,\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon})\,.

(4.10)

By (
4.8
)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(z_{\varepsilon})+{\rm O}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\psi(\varepsilon)}{2}+{\rm O}(\varepsilon)

where in the second identity we have used (
3.22
). Hence
\lambda(\varepsilon)=(1-(1/n))\,\psi(\varepsilon)+{\rm O}(\varepsilon)
and (
4.7
) follows from (
4.6
).

Step three
: We prove the continuity of
\lambda
on
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. Let
\varepsilon_{j}\to\varepsilon_{*}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
as
j\to\infty
and set
h_{j}=u_{\varepsilon_{j}}-u_{\varepsilon_{*}}
. By the resolution formula (
3.8
) we have

\displaystyle|u_{\varepsilon_{j}}(x)-u_{\varepsilon_{*}}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}-\tau_{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)-\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{*}}-\tau_{\varepsilon_{*}}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|f_{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)-f_{\varepsilon_{*}}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{*}}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon_{*}\,e^{-(|x|-R_{0})/C\,\varepsilon_{*}}+\Big|\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}-\tau_{0}\Big)-\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{*}}-\tau_{0}\Big)\Big|

where we have used (
3.17
), (
3.9
) and (
A.16
). Similarly, since
\varepsilon_{j}\to\varepsilon_{*}>0
, for
j
large enough we see that

\displaystyle\Big|\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}-\tau_{0}\Big)-\eta\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{*}}-\tau_{0}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\,\Big|\eta^{\prime}\Big(\frac{|x|-R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{*}+t(\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{*})}-\tau_{0}\Big)\Big|\,\frac{||x|-R_{0}|}{(\varepsilon_{*}+t\,(\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{*}))^{2}}\,|\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{*}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\frac{|\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{*}|}{\varepsilon_{*}^{2}}\,e^{-(|x|-R_{0})/C\,\varepsilon_{*}}\,||x|-R_{0}|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{*}\,e^{-(|x|-R_{0})/C\,\varepsilon_{*}}\,.

Setting
h_{j}=u_{\varepsilon_{j}}-u_{\varepsilon_{*}}
we see that (
4.1
), (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) hold with
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{*}
and for
j
large enough, thus deducing that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon_{*}\,|\nabla h_{j}|^{2}+\frac{h_{j}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{*}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{*}}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}})-\psi(\varepsilon_{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{\varepsilon_{*}},\frac{\varepsilon_{*}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big\}\,\psi(\varepsilon_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{*})\,.

From the continuity of
\psi
on
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
(Theorem
2.1
) we conclude that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon_{j}}-\nabla u_{\varepsilon_{*}}|^{2}=0\,,\qquad\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon_{j}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon_{*}})\,,

and thus
\lambda
is continuous on
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
thanks to (
4.10
).
∎

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
4.1
. This is based on a series of three lemmas, each containing a different stability estimate, coming increasingly closer to (
4.4
).

Lemma 4.3

(First stability lemma)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfy (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), and let

Q(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}2\,(u^{\prime})^{2}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,u^{2}\,,\qquad u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\,.

Then
Q(u)\geq 0
on
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, and
Q(u)=0
if and only if
u=t\,\eta^{\prime}
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the variational problem

\gamma=\inf\Big\{Q(u):\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}=1\Big\}\,.

By (
A.18
) we have
\eta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Differentiating
2\,\eta^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime}(\eta)
we find
2\,(\eta^{\prime})^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,\eta^{\prime}
, and then integration by parts gives
Q(\eta^{\prime})=0
. At the same time we clearly have
Q(u)\geq-\|W^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}(0,1)}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}
for every
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, so that

-\|W^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}(0,1)}\leq\gamma\leq 0\,.

We now prove that
\gamma
is attained. Let
\{w_{j}\}_{j}
be a minimizing sequence for
\gamma
. By the concentration-compactness principle,
\{w_{j}^{2}\,dx\}_{j}
is in the vanishing case if

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{I_{R}}w_{j}^{2}=0\,,\qquad\forall R>0\,,

(4.11)

where we have set
I_{R}=(-R,R)
. By (
A.16
) and (
A.6
) there exists
S_{0}
such that

W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{S_{0}}$}\,.

(4.12)

Therefore by applying (
4.11
) twice with
R=S_{0}
we find

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{S_{0}}}w_{j}^{2}\leq C\,\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{S_{0}}}W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,w_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\,\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,w_{j}^{2}\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}Q(w_{j})=\gamma\leq 0

a contradiction to
\int_{\mathbb{R}}w_{j}^{2}=1
. If, instead,
\{w_{j}^{2}\,dx\}_{j}
is in the dichotomy case, then there is
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that for every
\tau\in(0,\alpha/2)
there exist
R>0
and
R_{j}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
such that

\Big|1-\alpha-\int_{I_{R}}w_{j}^{2}\Big|<\tau\,,\qquad\Big|\alpha-\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{R_{j}}}w_{j}^{2}\Big|<\tau\,,

(4.13)

where, without loss of generality, we can assume
R\geq S_{0}
for
S_{0}
as in (
4.12
). In particular, if
\varphi
is a cut-off function between
I_{R}
and
I_{R_{j}}
, then we have

Q(w_{j})=Q(\varphi\,w_{j})+Q\big((1-\varphi)\,w_{j}\big)+E_{j}\,,

(4.14)

where, taking into account that
\varphi^{\prime}
and
(1-\varphi)\,\varphi
are supported in
I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{j}=2\,\int_{I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}}W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,(1-\varphi)\,\varphi\,w_{j}^{2}+4\,\int_{I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}}(\varphi w_{j})^{\prime}((1-\varphi)w_{j})^{\prime}\,.

(4.15)

The first integral in (
4.15
) is non-negative by (
4.12
), while the second integral contains a non-negative term of the form
\varphi\,(1-\varphi)\,(w_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
: therefore, by (
4.13
),

\displaystyle E_{j}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 4\,\int_{I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}}w_{j}\,\,w_{j}^{\prime}\,(1-\varphi)\,\varphi^{\prime}-w_{j}\,w_{j}^{\prime}\,\varphi\,\varphi^{\prime}-w_{j}^{2}\,(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-C\,\int_{I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}}w_{j}^{2}-C\,\Big(\int_{I_{R_{j}}\setminus I_{R}}w_{j}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(w_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\geq-C\,\sqrt{\tau}\,,

where we have also used
Q(w_{j})\to\gamma
as
j\to\infty
to infer

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(w_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\leq Q(w_{j})+\|W\|_{C^{0}[0,1]}\leq C\,.

We can take
\varphi
supported in
I_{R+1}
. In this way, up to extracting a subsequence, we have that
\varphi\,w_{j}
admits a weak limit
w
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. By lower semicontinuity, homogeneity of
Q
and (
4.13
) we have that

\liminf_{j\to\infty}Q(\varphi\,w_{j})\geq Q(w)\geq\gamma\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}w^{2}\geq\,(1-\alpha)\,\gamma-C\,\tau\,.

(4.17)

Finally, since
(1-\varphi)
is supported on
\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{S_{0}}
, by (
4.12
) we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q\big((1-\varphi)\,v_{j}\big)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,(1-\varphi)^{2}\,w_{j}^{2}\geq\frac{\alpha}{C}-C\,\tau\,,

so that, combining (
4.14
), (
4.16
), and (
4.17
) we find

\gamma\geq(1-\alpha)\,\gamma+\frac{\alpha}{C}-C\,\sqrt{\tau}\,.

Letting
\tau\to 0^{+}
we find a contradiction with
\gamma\leq 0
and
\alpha>0
. Having excluded vanishing and dichotomy, we have proved the existence of minimizers of
\gamma
.

Let now
u
be a minimizer of
\gamma
. Up to replace with
u
with
|u|
we can assume
u\geq 0
. By a standard variational argument there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}2u^{\prime}\,v^{\prime}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,u\,v=\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}u\,v\,,\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\,.

(4.18)

Testing with
v=\eta^{\prime}
and recalling that
2\,(\eta^{\prime})^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\eta^{\prime}
, we deduce that

\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{\prime}u=0,

and, since
u\geq 0
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}=1
, and
\eta^{\prime}<0
, we find
\lambda=0
. Thus
u
is a
C^{2}
-solution of the ODE
2\,u^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\,u
on
\mathbb{R}
. If
u(r_{0})=0
for some
r_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, then
u^{\prime}(r_{0})\neq 0
(otherwise we would have
u=0
on
\mathbb{R}
, against
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}=1
), and
u^{\prime}(r_{0})\neq 0
contradicts
u\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
u>0
on
\mathbb{R}
.

Having proved that every minimizer of
\gamma
is either positive or negative on the whole
\mathbb{R}
, we deduce that
\gamma=0
. Indeed, if
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are minimizers of
\gamma
and, say, they are both positive on
\mathbb{R}
, then they solve (
4.18
) with
\lambda=0
, and thus

2\,\gamma=Q(u_{1})+Q(u_{2})=Q(u_{1}-u_{2})\geq\gamma\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}(u_{1}-u_{2})^{2}=2\,\gamma\,\Big(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{1}\,u_{2}\Big)\,.

Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{1}\,u_{2}\in(0,1)
,
\gamma<0
would give a contradiction. Having established that
\gamma=0
, we now know that
\eta^{\prime}
is a minimizer of
\gamma
. If
u
is also minimizer of
\gamma
, then, again by (
4.18
),

Q(u+s\,\eta^{\prime})=Q(u)+s^{2}\,Q(\eta^{\prime})=0\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}\,.

In particular, if
s\in\mathbb{R}
is such that
u+s\,\eta^{\prime}
is not identically zero on
\mathbb{R}
, then
(u+s\,\eta^{\prime})/\|u+s\,\eta^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}
is a minimizer of
\gamma
, and thus
u+s\,\eta^{\prime}
is either positive or negative on the whole
\mathbb{R}
. Let
s_{0}=\inf\{s:\mbox{$u+s\,\eta^{\prime}<0$ on $\mathbb{R}$}\}
. If, say,
u
is a negative minimizer (like
\eta^{\prime}
is), then
s_{0}\leq 0
; while, clearly,
s_{0}>-\infty
, since, for
s
negative enough, we must have
u+s\,\eta^{\prime}>0
at at least one point, and thus everywhere. Since
u+s_{0}\,\eta^{\prime}\leq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
with
u+s_{0}\,\eta^{\prime}=0
at at least one point, we deduce that
u+s_{0}\,\eta^{\prime}=0
on
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4

(Second stability lemma)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}
with the following property. If
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
is a minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a radial function such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h=0\,,

(4.19)

then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,h^{2}\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,,

(4.20)

where
\lambda(\varepsilon)
is the Lagrange multiplier of
u_{\varepsilon}
as in (
4.5
).

Proof.

Step one
: We show that is enough to prove the lemma with

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,h^{2}\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,,

(4.21)

in place of (
4.20
). Indeed, if
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough, then
|\lambda(\varepsilon)|\leq c(n)
thanks to (
2.3
), and thus we can find a universal constant
C_{*}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})-\lambda(\varepsilon)V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big|\,h^{2}\leq C_{*}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,,

whenever
u_{\varepsilon}
is a minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
,
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, and
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let us now fix a radial function
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying (
4.19
). If
C_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}/\varepsilon\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}
, then we trivially have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V^{\prime\prime}(\zeta_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,h^{2}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon|\nabla h|^{2}-C_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}\,;

if, instead,
C_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}/\varepsilon\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}
, then we deduce from (
4.21
)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,h^{2}\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\,\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{1}{C\,C_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}\,.

In both cases, (
4.20
) is easily deduced.

Step two
: We prove (
4.21
). We argue by contradiction, and consider
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
as
j\to\infty
,
u_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
minimizers of
\psi(\varepsilon_{j})
, and radial functions
h_{j}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{j})\,h_{j}=0\,,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon_{j}\,|\nabla h_{j}|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{j})}{\varepsilon_{j}}-\lambda_{j}\,V^{\prime\prime}(u_{j})\Big)\,h_{j}^{2}<\frac{1}{j}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h_{j}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,.

(4.23)

where
\lambda_{j}
are the Lagrange multipliers corresponding to
u_{j}
. By homogeneity of (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) we can also assume that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{h_{j}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{j}}=1\,.

(4.24)

Therefore, setting

\eta_{j}(s)=u_{j}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)\,,\qquad\beta_{j}(s)=h_{j}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)\,,\qquad s\geq-\frac{R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,,

we can recast (
4.23
) and (
4.24
) as

\displaystyle\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\Big(2\,(\beta_{j}^{\prime})^{2}+(W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})-\varepsilon_{j}\,\lambda_{j}\,V^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j}))\,\beta_{j}^{2}\Big)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,\leq\frac{1}{j}\,,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds=1\,.

(4.26)

By
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
and by (
2.3
) we know
\lambda_{j}\to c(n)
as
j\to\infty
, which combined with
\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}[0,1]}\leq C
and
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
shows that (
4.25
) and (
4.26
) imply

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\Big\{2\,(\beta_{j}^{\prime})^{2}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\,\beta_{j}^{2}\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq 0\,.

(4.27)

Since
W^{\prime\prime}
is bounded on
[0,1]
, by (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) we deduce that
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j}
is bounded in
H^{1}(-s_{0},s_{0})
for every
s_{0}>0
. In particular there exists
\beta\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R})
such that, up to extracting subsequences,
\beta
is the weak limit of
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j}
in
H^{1}(-s_{0},s_{0})
for every
s_{0}>0
. By
\beta_{j}^{\prime}\rightharpoonup\beta^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(-s_{0},s_{0})
for every
s_{0}>0
we easily find

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to\infty}\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}2\,\beta_{j}^{\prime}(s)^{2}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\geq R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}2\,(\beta^{\prime})^{2}\,.

(4.28)

We now apply the concentration-compactness principle to the sequence of measures

\mu_{j}=1_{(-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j},\infty)}(s)\,\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,,

which satisfy
\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R})=1
thanks to (
4.24
). We claim that, if the compactness case hold, and thus

\lim_{s_{0}\to+\infty}\sup_{j}\,\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-s_{0},s_{0}])=0\,,

(4.29)

then we can reach a contradiction, and complete the proof of the lemma. To prove this claim, let us set

\eta_{0}(s)=\eta(s-\tau_{0})\,,

for
\tau_{0}
as in (
A.19
), and let us notice that, for every
s_{0}>0
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Big|\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds-R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{0})\,\beta^{2}\Big|

(4.30)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{-s_{0}}^{s_{0}}\Big|W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}-R_{0}^{n-1}\,W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{0})\,\beta^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle+\|W^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}[0,1]}\,\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-s_{0},s_{0}])+R_{0}^{n-1}\,\|W^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}[0,1]}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-s_{0},s_{0}]}\beta^{2}\,.

Since
\beta_{j}\to\beta
in
L^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\eta_{j}\to\eta_{0}
locally uniformly on
\mathbb{R}
thanks to Theorem
3.1
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.30
) is equal to zero. Letting now
s_{0}\to\infty
, the second term goes to zero thanks to (
4.29
), while the third terms goes to zero thanks to the fact that (
4.29
) implies in particular

R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\beta^{2}=1\,.

(4.31)

We can combine this information with (
4.28
) and finally deduce from (
4.27
) that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,2\,(\beta^{\prime})^{2}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{0})\,\beta^{2}\leq 0\,.

(4.32)

By Lemma
4.3
we deduce that, if we set
\beta_{0}(s)=\beta(s+\tau_{0})
, then
\beta_{0}=t\,\eta^{\prime}
for some
t\neq 0
(
t=0
being ruled out by (
4.31
)). In particular,
\beta=t\,\eta_{0}^{\prime}
, and therefore

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,V^{\prime}(\eta_{0})\,\beta=t\,V(\eta_{0})|^{+\infty}_{-\infty}=t\,V(1)=t\neq 0\,.

However, by (
4.22
), we see that

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{j})\,h_{j}=\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\,V^{\prime}(\eta_{j})\,\beta_{j}(s)\,(R_{0}+s\,\varepsilon_{j})^{n-1}\,ds\,,\qquad\forall j\,,

and we can thus obtain a contradiction by showing that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\,V^{\prime}(\eta_{j})\,\beta_{j}(s)\,(R_{0}+s\,\varepsilon_{j})^{n-1}\,ds=R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,V^{\prime}(\eta_{0})\,\beta\,.

(4.33)

This is proved by noticing that (
A.11
), (
A.16
), (
3.56
) and (
3.57
) give

0\leq\max\{V^{\prime}(\eta_{j}),V^{\prime}(\eta_{0})\}\leq C\,e^{-|s|/C}\,,

for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
(or for every
s\geq-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}
, in the case of
\eta_{j}
). In particular,

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{0}\to\infty}\limsup_{j\to\infty}\,\Big[\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{-s_{0}}+\int_{s_{0}}^{\infty}\Big]V^{\prime}(\eta_{j})\,|\beta_{j}|\,(R_{0}+s\,\varepsilon_{j})^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\,C\,\lim_{s_{0}\to\infty}\,\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Big(\int_{\{|s|>s_{0}\}}e^{-|s|/C}\,(R_{0}+s\,\varepsilon_{j})^{n-1}\,ds\Big)^{1/2}\,\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-s_{0},s_{0}])^{1/2}=0\,,

so that a similar argument to the one used in (
4.30
) can be repeated to prove (
4.33
).

We are thus left to prove that the sequence of probability measures
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j}
cannot be in the vanishing case nor in the dichotomy case of the concentration-compactness principle.

To exclude that
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j}
is in the vanishing case
: Since
\eta_{j}\to\eta
locally uniformly on
\mathbb{R}
, up to take
j
large enough and for
S_{0}
as in (
4.12
) we have
W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j}(s))\geq 1/C
for
|s|\geq S_{0}
,
s\geq-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}
. Since we are in the vanishing case, it holds

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{-S_{0}}^{S_{0}}\,\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds=0\,,

(4.34)

so that, by using first the lower bound on
W^{\prime\prime}
, and then (
4.34
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\,\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Big[\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{-S_{0}}+\int_{S_{0}}^{\infty}\Big]\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Big[\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{-S_{0}}+\int_{S_{0}}^{\infty}\Big]W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\,\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\,W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\,\beta_{j}(s)^{2}\,\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq 0

where in the last inequality we have used (
4.27
). Combining this information with (
4.34
) we obtain a contradiction to (
4.26
), thus excluding the vanishing case.

To exclude that
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j}
is in the dichotomy case
: With
S_{0}
as above, if we are in the dichotomy case, then there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that for every
\tau\in(0,\alpha/2)
there exist
R>S_{0}
and
R_{j}\to\infty
such that

|\mu_{j}(I_{R})-(1-\alpha)|<\tau\,,\qquad|\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{R_{j}})-\alpha|<\tau\,,\qquad\forall j\,.

(4.35)

Setting
A_{j}=\varphi\,\beta_{j}
,
B_{j}=(1-\varphi)\,\beta_{j}
, where
\varphi
is a cut-off function between
B_{R}
and
B_{R+1}
, and setting for the sake of brevity,

Q_{j}(A,B)=\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\Big\{2\,A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\,A\,B\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,,\qquad Q_{j}(A)=Q_{j}(A,A)\,,

we can rewrite (
4.27
) as

\limsup_{j\to\infty}Q_{j}(A_{j})+Q_{j}(B_{j})+2\,Q_{j}(A_{j},B_{j})\leq 0\,,

(4.36)

Now, since
\varphi^{\prime}
and
(1-\varphi)\,\varphi
are supported in
I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R}
, we see that

\displaystyle Q_{j}(A_{j},B_{j})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,\int_{I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R}}\,(1-2\,\varphi)\,\varphi^{\prime}\,\beta_{j}\,\beta_{j}^{\prime}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R}}\,\Big\{W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})-(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}\Big\}\,\beta_{j}^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds

where, thanks to (
4.27
) and Hölder inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R}}\,(1-2\,\varphi)\,\varphi^{\prime}\,\beta_{j}\,\beta_{j}^{\prime}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq C\,\mu_{j}(I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R})^{1/2}\leq C\,\sqrt{\tau}\,

\displaystyle\int_{I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R}}\,\Big\{W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})-(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}\Big\}\,\beta_{j}^{2}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\leq C\,\mu_{j}(I_{R+1}\setminus I_{R})\leq C\,\tau\,.

We thus conclude that
Q_{j}(A_{j},B_{j})\geq-C\,\sqrt{\tau}
for every
j
, and thus, by (
4.36
), that

\limsup_{j\to\infty}Q_{j}(A_{j})+Q_{j}(B_{j})\leq C\,\sqrt{\tau}\,.

(4.37)

Now, since the supports of the
A_{j}
’s are uniformly bounded, we easily see that there exists
A\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
A_{j}\rightharpoonup A
weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
; in particular,

\liminf_{j\to\infty}Q_{j}(A_{j})\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,2\,(A^{\prime})^{2}+W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{0})\,A^{2}\geq 0\,,

where in the last inequality we have used Lemma
4.3
. By combining this last inequality with (
4.37
),
W^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{j})\geq 1/C
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{S_{0}}
, and
R\geq S_{0}
, we conclude that

C\,\sqrt{\tau}\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}Q_{j}(B_{j})\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon_{j}}^{\infty}\,(1-\varphi)^{2}\,\beta_{j}^{2}\,(R+s\,\varepsilon_{j})^{n-1}\,ds

and thus, by (
4.35
), that
C\,\sqrt{\tau}\geq(\alpha/C)-C\,\tau
. Letting
\tau\to 0^{+}
we obtain a contradiction with
\alpha>0
.
∎

Lemma 4.5

(Third stability lemma)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exist universal constants
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that, if
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
is a minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
is a radial function with

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1\,,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(u-u_{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\|u-u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\delta_{0}\,,

(4.40)

then, setting
h=u-u_{\varepsilon}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,h^{2}\geq\frac{1}{C}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,.

(4.41)

where
\lambda(\varepsilon)
is the Lagrange multiplier of
u_{\varepsilon}
as in (
4.5
).

Proof.

It will be convenient to set

\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon}(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+\frac{u\,v}{\varepsilon}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\varepsilon}(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+\Big(\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,u\,v\,,

as well as
P_{\varepsilon}(u)=P_{\varepsilon}(u,u)
and
Q_{\varepsilon}(u)=Q_{\varepsilon}(u,u)
. Let us start noticing that by Theorem
3.1
we have

\lim_{\sigma\to 0}\,\sup_{\varepsilon<\sigma}\,\sup_{v_{\varepsilon}}\,\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(v_{\varepsilon})\,v_{\varepsilon}-R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}V^{\prime}(\eta)\,\eta\Big|=0\,,

where
v_{\varepsilon}
runs over all radial minimizers of
\psi(\varepsilon)
. Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}V^{\prime}(\eta)\,\eta
is a positive constant depending on
n
and
W
only this shows in particular that

\frac{1}{C}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,u_{\varepsilon}\leq C\,,\qquad\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\,.

(4.42)

By (
4.42
), given
h=u-u_{\varepsilon}
as in the statement, we can always find
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,(h+t\,u_{\varepsilon})=0\,,\qquad\mbox{i.e.}\quad t=-\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,u_{\varepsilon}}\,.

(4.43)

By (
A.12
), (
4.40
), and since
0\leq u_{\varepsilon}+h\leq 1
, we have that, on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\Big|V(u_{\varepsilon}+h)-V(u_{\varepsilon})-V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h-V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,\delta_{0}\,h^{2}\,,

(4.44)

so that, by (
4.38
),

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h+V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,\delta_{0}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}\,,

(4.45)

and thus, thanks to
\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{0}[0,1]}\leq C
, (
4.42
), (
4.39
), and (
4.43
),

|t|\leq C\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,\min\{P_{\varepsilon}(h),1\}\,,

(4.46)

By (
4.43
) we can apply Lemma
4.4
to
u_{\varepsilon}+t\,h
and find that

Q_{\varepsilon}(h+t\,u_{\varepsilon})\geq\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(h+t\,u_{\varepsilon})}{C}\,,

which can be more conveniently rewritten as

Q_{\varepsilon}(h)\geq\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(h)}{C}+2\,t\,\Big\{\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})}{C}-Q_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}+t^{2}\,\Big\{\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})}{C}-Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,.

(4.47)

By Theorem
3.1
, we see that
P_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})+|Q_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})|\leq C
(uniformly on
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
), so that (
4.47
) and (
4.46
) give

Q_{\varepsilon}(h)\geq\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(h)}{C}+2\,t\,\Big\{\frac{P_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})}{C}-Q_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,.

(4.48)

By Hölder inequality,
ab\leq(a^{2}+b^{2})/2
,
P_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq C
, and (
4.46
) we see that

|t|\,P_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})\leq\frac{|t|}{2}\,\big(P_{\varepsilon}(h)+P_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\big)\leq C\,\varepsilon\,P_{\varepsilon}(h)\,,

(4.49)

while by
|V^{\prime}|+|W^{\prime\prime}|\leq C
and
|\lambda(\varepsilon)|\leq C
for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
we find, arguing as in (
4.49
),

|t|\,Q_{\varepsilon}(h,u_{\varepsilon})\leq|t|\,\Big\{\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla h|\,|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|\,u_{\varepsilon}\Big\}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,P_{\varepsilon}(h)\,.

(4.50)

By combining (
4.48
), (
4.49
), and (
4.50
) we conclude that
Q_{\varepsilon}(h)\geq P_{\varepsilon}(h)/C
, as desired.
∎

We are finally ready to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We are given
u_{\varepsilon}
and
h
as in Lemma
4.5
, and now want to prove that

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}+h)-\psi(\varepsilon)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,,

(4.51)

holds. By (
A.5
) and (
4.40
) we have that

\Big|W(u_{\varepsilon}+h)-W(u_{\varepsilon})-W^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h-W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,\delta_{0}\,h^{2}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,,

therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}+h)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla h+\frac{W^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}\,h

(4.52)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{2\,\varepsilon}\,h^{2}-C\,\delta_{0}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}\,.

By the Euler–Lagrange equation for
u_{\varepsilon}
, see (
2.1
), we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla h+\frac{W^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}\,h=\lambda(\varepsilon)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,h\,.

(4.53)

Moreover, by (
4.45
),

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})h+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\frac{h^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,\delta_{0}\,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}\,.

(4.54)

On combining (
4.52
), (
4.53
), and (
4.54
) with (
4.41
) we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}+h)-\psi(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\Big\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,W^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,h^{2}-C\,\delta_{0}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon\,|\nabla h|^{2}+\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}-C\,\delta_{0}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h^{2}\,,

so that (
4.51
) follows by taking
\delta_{0}
small enough.
∎

5.
Proof of the uniform stability theorem

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
-(iii), i.e., we prove (
1.21
). We focus directly on the case
(\sigma,m)=(\varepsilon,1)
, from which the general case follows immediately by scaling.

Theorem 5.1
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exist universal constants
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C
such that, if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
, then

C\,\sqrt{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)-\psi(\varepsilon)}\geq\inf_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})\big|^{n/(n-1)}

(5.1)

where
T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon}(x)=u_{\varepsilon}(x-x_{0})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
u_{\varepsilon}
denotes the unique minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
.

In order to streamline the exposition of the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we introduce the isoperimetric deficit and asymmetry of
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
, by setting

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)-\psi(\varepsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})\,.

Here, as in Theorem
2.2
,

d_{\Phi}(u,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|^{n/(n-1)}\,,\qquad\forall u,v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\,.

With this notation, Theorem
5.1
states the existence of universal constants
C
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that, if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then

C\,\sqrt{\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)}\geq\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\,,\qquad\forall u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\,,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1\,.

(5.2)

In the following subsections we discuss some key steps of the proof of Theorem
5.1
, which is then presented at the end of this section.

5.1.
Reduction to the small asymmetry case

Thanks to the volume constraint
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
and to the triangular inequality in
L^{n/(n-1)}
, we always have
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq 2^{n/(n-1)}
. In particular, in proving (
5.2
), we can always assume that
\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\delta_{0}
for a universal constant
\delta_{0}
. This is useful because, by the following lemma, by assuming
\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\delta_{0}
we can take
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)
as small as needed in dependence of
n
and
W
.

Lemma 5.2

(
\varepsilon
-uniform qualitative stability)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}
with the following property: for every
\alpha>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that

u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1\,,\qquad\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\,,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\delta\,,

imply

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\alpha\,.

Proof.

We pick
\varepsilon_{0}
such that Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
4.2
hold. If the lemma is false for such
\varepsilon_{0}
, then there exists
\alpha_{*}>0
and a sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u_{j})=1
such that

\delta_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\to 0^{+}\qquad\mbox{as $j\to\infty$}\,,

(5.3)

for some
\varepsilon_{j}\to\varepsilon_{*}\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and with
\alpha_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\geq\alpha_{*}
. By (
5.3
), there is
\ell_{j}\to 0^{+}
as
j\to\infty
such that

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\leq\psi(\varepsilon_{j})+\ell_{j}\,,\qquad\forall j\,,

(5.4)

We now distinguish two cases:

Case one,
\varepsilon_{*}>0
: In this case, by continuity of
\psi
(see Theorem
2.1
) and since

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{*}}(u_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{*})\leq b_{j}\,\big(\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})\big)+b_{j}\,\psi(\varepsilon_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{*})\,,\qquad b_{j}=\max\Big\{\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{\varepsilon_{*}},\frac{\varepsilon_{*}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big\}\,,

we can assume that
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{*}}(u_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{*})\leq\ell_{0}
for
\ell_{0}
as in step two of the proof of Theorem
2.1
. We can thus apply that statement and conclude that, up to translations and up to subsequences, there is
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
such that
d_{\Phi}(u_{j},u)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. In particular,
u
is a minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon_{*})
, and therefore, up to a translation, we can assume that
u=u_{\varepsilon_{*}}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Now, by repeating this same argument with the minimizers
u_{\varepsilon_{j}}
of
\psi(\varepsilon_{j})
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
in place of
u_{j}
, we see that

d_{\Phi}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},u_{\varepsilon_{*}})\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $j\to\infty$}\,,

so that, thanks to (
2.59
), we find the contradiction

\alpha_{*}\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\leq d_{\Phi}(u_{j},u_{\varepsilon_{j}})\leq d_{\Phi}(u_{j},u_{\varepsilon_{*}})+C\,d_{\Phi}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},u_{\varepsilon_{*}})^{(n-1)/n}\to 0^{+}\,,

as
j\to\infty
.

Case two,
\varepsilon_{*}=0
: In this case, thanks to (
5.4
),

2\,|D[\Phi(u_{j})]|(\mathbb{R}^{n})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\leq\psi(\varepsilon_{j})+\ell_{j}\leq 2\,n\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}+C\,\varepsilon_{j}+\ell_{j}\,,

so that
\{\Phi(u_{j})\}_{j}
is asymptotically optimal for the sharp
BV
-Sobolev inequality. By the concentration-compactness principle
(see, e.g.,
[
FMP07
, Theorem A.1]
), up to subsequences and up to translations,
\Phi(u_{j})\to a\,1_{B_{r}}
in
L^{n/(n-1)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
j\to\infty
, for some
a
and
r
such that
a^{n/(n-1)}\,\omega_{n}\,r^{n}=1
. The fact that
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})
is bounded implies that
v_{j}\to\{0,1\}
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, therefore, by
\Phi(0)=0
and
\Phi(1)=1
, it must be
a=1
and
R=R_{0}
for
\omega_{n}\,R_{0}^{n}=1
. By Theorem
3.1
, if
u_{\varepsilon_{j}}
is a the minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon_{j})
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, then

d_{\Phi}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},1_{B_{R_{0}}})\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $j\to\infty$}\,,

which gives the contradiction

\alpha_{*}\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\leq d_{\Phi}(u_{j},u_{\varepsilon_{j}})\leq d_{\Phi}(u_{j},1_{B_{R_{0}}})+C\,d_{\Phi}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},1_{B_{R_{0}}})^{(n-1)/n}\to 0^{+}\,,

as
j\to\infty
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
5.1
in the radial decreasing case

We start by noticing that, thanks to the results proved in the previous sections, we can quickly prove Theorem
5.1
for functions in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exist universal constants
C
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, denoting by
u_{\varepsilon}
the unique minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, one has

C\,\sqrt{\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)}\geq d_{\Phi}(u,u_{\varepsilon})\,,

(5.5)

whenever
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\cap\mathcal{R}_{0}
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
.

Proof.

Arguing by contradiction, we can find
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
and
\{v_{j}\}_{j}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\cap\mathcal{R}_{0}
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(v_{j})=1\,,\qquad a_{j}=\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})}{d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})^{2}}\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $j\to\infty$}\,,

where
u_{j}=u_{\varepsilon_{j}}
and, thanks to Lemma
5.2
and to
a_{j}\to 0^{+}
, we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})=0\,.

(5.6)

Correspondingly we consider the variational problems

\gamma_{j}=\gamma(\varepsilon_{j},a_{j},v_{j})=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w)+a_{j}\,d_{\Phi}(w,v_{j}):w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])\,,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(w)=1\Big\}\,.

With
a_{0}
,
\ell_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
as in Theorem
2.2
, we notice that, for
j
large enough, we have
a_{j}\in(0,a_{0})
,
\varepsilon_{j}<\varepsilon_{0}
, and

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})\leq\psi(\varepsilon_{j})+a_{j}\,\ell_{0}\,,\qquad d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})\leq\ell_{0}\,.

(5.7)

In particular we can apply Theorem
2.2
, and deduce the existence of minimizers
w_{j}
of
\gamma_{j}
. We claim that, as
j\to\infty
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})}{d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j})^{2}}=0\,.

(5.8)

To show this, we first notice that, by comparing
w_{j}
to
u_{j}
we have

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})+a_{j}\,d_{\Phi}(w_{j},v_{j})\leq\psi(\varepsilon_{j})+a_{j}\,d_{\Phi}(u_{j},v_{j})\,,

so that (
5.6
) gives
\delta_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})\to 0
, and then Lemma
5.2
implies

\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j})=0\,.

(5.9)

Next, comparing
w_{j}
to
v_{j}
we find that

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})+a_{j}\,d_{\Phi}(w_{j},v_{j})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})\,,

so that
\psi(\varepsilon_{j})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})
and the definition of
a_{j}
give

d_{\Phi}(w_{j},v_{j})\leq\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})}{a_{j}}=d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})^{2}\,.

(5.10)

By (
2.59
), (
5.6
), (
5.9
), and (
5.10
) we find

\displaystyle\big|d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j})-d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\max\big\{d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j}),d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})\big\}^{1/n}\,d_{\Phi}(w_{j},v_{j})^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle=
