\displaystyle{\rm o}\big(d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})^{2(n-1)/n}\big)\,,

where
2(n-1)/n\geq 1
thanks to
n\geq 2
. Thus,
d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j})\geq d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})/C
for
j
large enough, and
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})
gives

\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})}{d_{\Phi}(w_{j},u_{j})^{2}}\leq C\,\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(v_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})}{d_{\Phi}(v_{j},u_{j})^{2}}\to 0^{+}\,,

as claimed in (
5.8
).

We now derive a contradiction to (
5.8
). By Theorem
2.2
, we know that
w_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}\cap C^{2,1/(n-1)}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
0<w_{j}<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

-2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Delta w_{j}=\varepsilon_{j}\,w_{j}\,(1-w_{j})\,\mathrm{E}_{j}-W^{\prime}(w_{j})\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,,

(5.11)

where
\mathrm{E}_{j}
is a continuous radial function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\mathrm{E}_{j}|\leq C\,.

(5.12)

We can thus apply Theorem
3.1
to
w_{j}
. In particular, since both
u_{j}
and
w_{j}
obey the resolution formula (
3.8
), we have that
h_{j}=w_{j}-u_{j}
satisfies

|h_{j}(R_{0}+\varepsilon_{j}\,s)|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,e^{-|s|/C}\qquad\forall s\geq-\frac{R_{0}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,.

(5.13)

In particular,

\|h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h_{j}^{2}\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}\,.

and we can thus apply Theorem
4.1
to deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})-\psi(\varepsilon_{j})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varepsilon_{j}\,|\nabla h_{j}|^{2}+\frac{h_{j}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{j}}

(5.14)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(h_{j}^{2})|\geq\frac{1}{C}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h_{j}|^{2n/(n-1)}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,,

where we have also used the
BV
-Sobolev inequality. By (
5.13
), and by applying (
3.14
) to
u_{j}
in combination with (
A.6
), we find that, if
A_{j}=B_{R_{0}+c_{j}}\setminus B_{R_{0}-b_{j}}
, then, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A_{j}
we have

|\Phi(u_{j}(x))-\Phi(w_{j}(x))|\leq|h_{j}(x)|\,\int_{0}^{1}\,\sqrt{W(u_{j}(x)+t\,h_{j}(x))}\,dt\leq C\,|h_{j}(x)|\,e^{-||x|-R_{0}|/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}\,,

and, therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A_{j}}|\Phi(u_{j})-\Phi(w_{j})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A_{j}}|h_{j}|^{n/(n-1)}\,e^{-||x|-R_{0}|/C\,\varepsilon_{j}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,C\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h_{j}|^{2\,n/(n-1)}\Big)^{1/2}\,.

If, instead,
x\in A_{j}
, then by
|\Phi(u_{j})-\Phi(w_{j})|\leq C\,|h_{j}|
and
\mathcal{L}^{n}(A_{j})\leq C\,\varepsilon_{j}
we find

\int_{A_{j}}|\Phi(u_{j})-\Phi(w_{j})|^{n/(n-1)}\leq C\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h_{j}|^{2\,n/(n-1)}\Big)^{1/2}\,.

(5.16)

By combining (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and (
5.16
), and thanks to
\varepsilon_{j}\leq 1
,
n/(n-1)\geq 1
, and
\delta_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})\leq 1
, we conclude that

d_{\Phi}(u_{j},w_{j})\leq C\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}\,\delta_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})^{n/2\,(n-1)}\leq C\,\sqrt{\delta_{\varepsilon_{j}}(w_{j})}\,,

in contradiction to (
5.8
).
∎

Remark 5.1
.

The argument we have just presented provides further indication that (
5.5
) should not provide a sharp rate on radial decreasing functions. The sharp stability estimate on small radial perturbations of
u_{\varepsilon}
is clearly given in Theorem
4.1
, but it is not clear what form the sharp stability estimate should take on
\mathcal{R}_{0}
(or, more generally, on arbitrary radial functions).

5.3.
Reduction to radial decreasing functions

We now discuss the reduction of (
5.2
) to the case of radial decreasing functions. We do this by adapting to our setting the “quantitative symmetrization” strategy developed in
[
FMP08
,
FMP07
]
in the study of Euclidean isoperimetry.

Given
n\geq 2
and
k\in\{1,...,n\}
we say that
u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is
k
-symmetric
if there exists
k
mutually orthogonal hyperplanes such that
u
is symmetric by reflection through each of these hyperplanes. The class of
n
-symmetric functions is particularly convenient when it comes to quantify sharp inequalities involving radial decreasing rearrangements. Consider for example the Pólya-Szegö inequality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u^{*}|^{2}\,,

(5.17)

where
u^{*}
is the radial decreasing rearrangement of
u
. A classical result of Brothers and Ziemer
[
BZ88
]
shows that equality can hold in (
5.17
) without
u
being a translation of
u^{*}
; in general, the additional condition that
(u^{*})^{\prime}<0
a.e. must be assumed to deduce symmetry from equality in (
5.17
) (compare with step six in the proof of Theorem
2.1
). However, if
u
is
n
-symmetric, then equality in (
5.17
) automatically implies that
u
is radial decreasing. A quantitative version of this statement is proved in
[
FMP07
, Theorem 2.2]
in the
BV
-case of (
5.17
), and in
[
CFMP09
, Theorem 3]
in the Sobolev case. The following theorem is an adaptation of those results to our setting.

Theorem 5.4

(Reduction from
n
-symmetric to radial decreasing functions)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exists a universal constant
C
with the following property. If
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
is a
n
-symmetric function with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
and
u^{*}
is its radial decreasing rearrangement, then

d_{\Phi}(u,u^{*})\leq C\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)\Big)^{1/2}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u^{*}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\,.

(5.18)

Moreover, for every
\varepsilon>0
we have

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C\,\Big(\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u^{*})+\big(\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\big)^{1/2}\,\Big)\,.

(5.19)

Proof.

We first claim that

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(u,u^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{n}{n-1}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{L}^{n}(E_{t})\Phi(t)^{1/(n-1)}\,\sqrt{W(t)}\,dt\,,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u^{*}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C(n)}\,\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{\mathcal{L}^{n}(E_{t})}{\mu(t)}\Big)^{2}\frac{\mu(t)^{2\,(n-1)/n}}{-\mu^{\prime}(t)}\,dt\,,

(5.21)

where
E_{t}=\{u>t\}\Delta\{u^{*}>t\}
,
\mu(t)=\mathcal{L}^{n}(\{u>t\})
, and
\mu^{\prime}(t)
denotes the absolutely continuous part of the distributional derivative of the decreasing function
\mu
. To prove (
5.20
) we recall that, by
[
CFMP09
, Lemma 5]
, we have

d_{\Phi}(u,u^{*})\leq\frac{n}{n-1}\,\int_{0}^{1}\,\mathcal{L}^{n}(F_{s})\,s^{1/(n-1)}\,ds\,,

provided
F_{s}=\{\Phi(u)>s\}\Delta\{\Phi(u^{*})>s\}
. Since
\Phi
is strictly increasing, we have
F_{\Phi(t)}=E_{t}
, so that the change of variables
s=\Phi(t)
gives (
5.20
). To prove (
5.21
) we just notice that this is
[
CFMP09
, Equation (3.18)]
. Now, by Hölder inequality and (
5.20
), we find that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{L}^{n}(E_{s})\,\Phi^{1/(n-1)}\,\sqrt{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{L}^{n}(E_{s})}{\mu}\,\frac{\mu^{(n-1)/n}}{(-\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}}\,\frac{(-\mu^{\prime})^{1/2}}{\mu^{-1/n}}\,\Phi^{1/(n-1)}\,\sqrt{W}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{\mathcal{L}^{n}(E_{s})}{\mu}\Big)^{2}\frac{\mu^{2\,(n-1)/n}}{-\mu^{\prime}}\Big)^{1/2}\,\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{-\mu^{\prime}}{\mu^{-2/n}}\,\Phi^{2/(n-1)}\,W\,\Big)^{1/2}\,.

By
1=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)\geq V(t)\,\mu(t)
for every
t\in(0,1)
, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{-\mu^{\prime}}{\mu^{-2/n}}\,\Phi^{2/(n-1)}\,W\leq\int_{0}^{1}-\mu^{\prime}\,\big(V\,\mu\big)^{2/n}\,W\leq\int_{0}^{1}\,-\mu^{\prime}\,W\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)\,,

where in the last inequality we have used
-\mu^{\prime}\,d\mathcal{L}^{1}\leq-D\mu
, integration by parts and Fubini’s theorem to deduce

-\int_{0}^{1}\,W\,d[D\mu]=\int_{0}^{1}\,W^{\prime}(t)\,\mu(t)\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\,dx\int_{0}^{u(x)}W^{\prime}(t)\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)\,.

By combining (
5.20
), (
5.21
) and these estimates we find (
5.18
). To prove (
5.19
), we notice that, by
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u^{*})
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u^{*})=1
, (
5.18
) gives

d_{\Phi}(u,u^{*})\leq C\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u^{*})\Big)^{1/2}\leq C\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)^{1/2}\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)^{1/2}

(5.22)

and then (
5.19
) follows by the triangular inequality in
L^{n/(n-1)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

Next we discuss the reduction from generic functions to
n
-symmetric ones.

Theorem 5.5

(Reduction to
n
-symmetric functions)
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exist universal constants
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta_{0}
with the following property. If
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
and
\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\delta_{0}
for some
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then there exists
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(v)=1
such that
v
is
n
-symmetric and

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v)\,,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}(v)\leq C\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\,.

(5.23)

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\delta_{0}
for a universal constant
\delta_{0}
. By Lemma
5.2
we can choose
\delta_{0}
so that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\alpha_{0}
for
\alpha_{0}
a universal constant of our choice. We divide the proof into a few steps.

Step one
: We prove that, if
u
is
k
-symmetric,
\{H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
are the mutually orthogonal hyperplanes of symmetry of
u
, and
J=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}H_{i}
, then

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u;J)=\inf_{x\in J}\,d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x}u_{\varepsilon})\leq C(n)\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\,.

(5.24)

In other words, in computing the asymmetry of
u
in the proof of an estimate like (
5.2
), we can compare
u
with a translation of
u_{\varepsilon}
with maximum on
J
.

Indeed, let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)=d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
x_{0}\not\in J
. In particular, if
y_{0}
denotes the reflection of
x_{0}
with respect to
J
, then
y_{0}\neq x_{0}
and

d_{\Phi}(u,T_{y_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})=d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})=\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\,,

(5.25)

that is, also
y_{0}
is an optimal center for computing
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)
. Let
z_{0}=(x_{0}+y_{0})/2
, so that
z_{0}\in J
, let
\nu=(x_{0}-y_{0})/|x_{0}-y_{0}|
(which is well defined by
x_{0}\neq y_{0}
), and let
H
be the open half-space orthogonal to
\nu
, containing
x_{0}
, and such that
z_{0}\in\partial H
.
By
T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon}(x)=u_{\varepsilon}(x-z_{0}-t\,\nu)
, we have that

\frac{d}{dt}\,T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon}(x)=-\nu\cdot\frac{x-z_{0}-t\,\nu}{|x-z_{0}-t\,\nu|}\,u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(|x-x_{0}-t\,\nu|)>0\,,\qquad\forall x\in H\,,t<0\,,

since
u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}<0
, and since the fact that
\nu
points inside
H
gives

\displaystyle(z-z_{0})\cdot\nu>0\,,\qquad\forall z\in H\,,

\displaystyle z=x-t\,\nu\in H\,,\qquad\forall x\in H\,,t<0\,.

We thus find that, if
t<0
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n}{n-1}\,\int_{H}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon})|^{1/(n-1)}\,\sqrt{W(T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon})}\,\frac{d}{dt}\,T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon}>0\,,

so that

\displaystyle\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u)-\Phi(T_{y_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{H^{-}}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\Big|_{t=-|x_{0}-y_{0}|/2}

(5.26)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}+t\,\nu}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

Now, since both
u
and
T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon}
are symmetric by reflection with respect to
\partial H
, we have that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}=2\,\int_{H}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\,,

(5.27)

therefore, by (
5.25
), (
5.26
) and (
5.27
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u;J)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(u,T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})=2\,\int_{H}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)\,\Big(\int_{H}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}+\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})-\Phi(T_{z_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)\,\Big(\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)+\int_{H}|\Phi(T_{y_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})-\Phi(T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)\,\Big(\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)+d_{\Phi}(T_{y_{0}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)\,\Big(\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)+d_{\Phi}(T_{y_{0}}u_{\varepsilon},u)+d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x_{0}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big)=C(n)\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\,,

that is (
5.24
).

Step two
: Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be two orthogonal hyperplanes through the origin, let
H_{i}^{\pm}
be the half-spaces defined by
H_{i}
, and let
x_{i}^{\pm}\in\partial H_{i}
. For
i=1,2
, consider the functions

U[u_{\varepsilon},H_{i},x_{i}^{+},x_{i}^{-}]=1_{H_{i}^{+}}\,T_{x_{i}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon}+1_{H_{i}^{-}}\,T_{x_{i}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}\,,

obtained by “gluing” the restriction of
u_{\varepsilon}
to
H_{i}^{+}
translated by
x_{1}^{+}
to the restriction of
u_{\varepsilon}
to
H_{i}^{-}
translated by
x_{1}^{+}
(notice that translating by
x_{i}^{\pm}
brings
H_{i}^{+}
and
H_{i}^{-}
into themselves). Setting for brevity

U_{\varepsilon,i}=U[u_{\varepsilon},H_{i},x_{i}^{+},x_{i}^{-}]

we claim that, for every
a\in(0,1)
there is
\kappa=\kappa(a,n,W)>0
such that if

\max\Big\{|x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}|,|x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}|,|x_{1}^{+}-x_{2}^{+}|\Big\}\leq\kappa\,,

(5.28)

then, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,

\max\Big\{d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}),d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\leq\frac{8}{1-a}\,d_{\Phi}(U_{\varepsilon,1},U_{\varepsilon,2})\,.

(5.29)

Indeed, since
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are hyperplanes through the origin and
u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, we have

\int_{H_{1}^{\pm}}V(T_{x_{1}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2}\,,\qquad\int_{H_{2}^{\pm}}V(T_{x_{2}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2}\,.

It is in general not true that, say,
H_{1}^{+}\cap H_{2}^{+}
has measure
1/4
for either
V(T_{x_{1}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})\,dx
or
V(T_{x_{1}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})\,dx
. However, provided we choose
\kappa
sufficiently small, thanks to Theorem
3.1
, we can definitely ensure that, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
\beta,\gamma\in\{+,-\}
, we have

\int_{H_{1}^{\beta}\cap H_{2}^{\gamma}}|\Phi(T_{x_{1}^{\beta}}u_{\varepsilon})-\Phi(T_{x_{2}^{\gamma}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\geq\frac{1-a}{4}\,d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{\beta}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{\gamma}}u_{\varepsilon})\,.

Correspondingly,

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(U_{\varepsilon,1},U_{\varepsilon,2})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{H_{1}^{\beta}\cap H_{2}^{\gamma}}|\Phi(U_{\varepsilon,1})-\Phi(U_{\varepsilon,2})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{H_{1}^{\beta}\cap H_{2}^{\gamma}}|\Phi(T_{x_{1}^{\beta}}u_{\varepsilon})-\Phi(T_{x_{2}^{\gamma}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}\geq\frac{1-a}{4}\,d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{\beta}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{\gamma}}u_{\varepsilon})\,,

and thus

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})^{(n-1)/n}+d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{8}{1-a}\,d_{\Phi}(U_{\varepsilon,1},U_{\varepsilon,2})\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,,

as claimed.

Step three
: Given
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
, we now consider an hyperplane
H
such that, if
H^{+}
and
H^{-}
denote the two open half-spaces defined by
H
, then

\int_{H^{+}}V(u)=\int_{H^{-}}V(u)=\frac{1}{2}\,.

Denoting by
\rho_{H}
the reflection with respect to
H
, we let

u^{+}=1_{H^{+}}\,u+1_{H^{-}}\,(u\circ\rho_{H})\,,\qquad u^{-}=1_{H^{-}}\,u+1_{H^{+}}\,(u\circ\rho_{H})\,,

(5.30)

and notice that
u^{\pm}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
, with

2\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u^{+})+\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u^{-})\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u^{+})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u^{-})=1\,.

(5.31)

We claim that

\max\{\delta_{\varepsilon}(u^{+}),\delta_{\varepsilon}(u^{-})\}\leq 2\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)\,,\qquad\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C(n)\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u^{+})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u^{-})+d_{\Phi}(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,,

(5.32)

provided
T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon}=
and
T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}=T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}
are such that
x^{+},x^{-}\in H
with

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u^{+};H)=d_{\Phi}(u^{+},T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})\,,\qquad\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u^{-};H)=d_{\Phi}(u^{-},T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\,.

The first inequality in (
5.32
) is obvious from (
5.31
). To prove the second one we notice that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(u,T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})=\int_{H^{+}}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}+\int_{H^{-}}|\Phi(u)-\Phi(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{H^{+}}|\Phi(u^{+})-\Phi(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}+\int_{H^{-}}|\Phi(u^{-})-\Phi(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})|^{n/(n-1)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)\,\Big\{d_{\Phi}(u^{+},T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})+d_{\Phi}(u^{-},T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})+d_{\Phi}(T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,,

that is the second inequality in (
5.32
).

With these preliminary considerations in place, we now prove that if
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V(u)=1
, if
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are orthogonal hyperplanes such that the corresponding half-spaces
H_{i}^{\pm}
satisfy

\int_{H_{i}^{\pm}}V(u)=\frac{1}{2}\,,

if
u_{i}^{\pm}
as in (
5.30
) starting from
H_{i}
, then there is at least one
v\in\{u_{1}^{+},u_{1}^{-},u_{2}^{+},u_{2}^{-}\}
such that (
5.23
) holds. Given that
\delta_{\varepsilon}(v)\leq 2\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(u)
for every
v\in\{u_{1}^{+},u_{1}^{-},u_{2}^{+},u_{2}^{-}\}
, we need to show that

\mbox{$\exists v\in\{u_{1}^{+},u_{1}^{-},u_{2}^{+},u_{2}^{-}\}$ such that $\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v)$}\,.

(5.33)

Denoting by
x_{i}^{\pm}
the points in
H_{i}
such that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{i}^{\pm};H_{i})=d_{\Phi}(u_{i}^{\pm},T_{x_{i}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})\,,

we notice that (
5.33
) follows if we can show that, provided
\alpha_{0}
is small enough, then

either

\displaystyle\qquad d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\leq M\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{+};H_{1})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{-};H_{1})\Big\}

(5.34)

or

\displaystyle\qquad d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\leq M\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{2}^{+};H_{2})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{2}^{-};H_{2})\Big\}\,,

(5.35)

for a constant
M
(as it turns out, any
M>16
works). Indeed, if, for example, (
5.34
) holds, then (
5.24
) and (
5.32
) with
H=H_{1}
give

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{+})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{-})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{+};H_{1})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{-};H_{1})\Big\}\leq C\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{+})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{-})\Big\}\,,

and then either
C\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{1}^{+})\geq\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)
or
C\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{2}^{+})\geq\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)
; in particular, (
5.33
) holds. We now want to prove that either (
5.34
) or (
5.35
) holds. We argue by contradiction. Recalling that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u_{i}^{\pm};H_{i})=d_{\Phi}(u_{i}^{\pm},T_{x_{i}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon})
, let us thus assume that both

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})>M\,\Big\{d_{\Phi}(u_{1}^{+},T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})+d_{\Phi}(u_{1}^{-},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,,

(5.36)

\displaystyle d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})>M\,\Big\{d_{\Phi}(u_{2}^{+},T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon})+d_{\Phi}(u_{2}^{-},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,,

(5.37)

hold for
M
to be determined. In particular, if
U_{\varepsilon,i}
,
i=1,2
, are defined as in step two, and
\alpha_{0}
is small enough that (
5.28
) holds, then, by (
5.29
), we have

\displaystyle\max\Big\{d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}),d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{8}{1-a}\,d_{\Phi}(U_{\varepsilon,1},U_{\varepsilon,2})\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\leq\Big(\frac{8}{1-a}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{2}d_{\Phi}(U_{\varepsilon,i},u)^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{8}{1-a}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{2}\,\Big(\sum_{\beta=+,-}\int_{H_{i}^{\beta}}|\Phi(T_{x_{i}^{\beta}}u_{\varepsilon})-\Phi(u_{i}^{\beta})|^{n/(n-1)}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{8}{M\,(1-a)}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{2}\,\Big(d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{i}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{i}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big)^{(n-1)/n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{16}{M\,(1-a)}\Big)^{(n-1)/n}\,\max\Big\{d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{1}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{1}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon}),d_{\Phi}(T_{x_{2}^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x_{2}^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}^{(n-1)/n}\,.

We fix
M>16
and apply the above with
a\in(0,1)
such that
M\,(1-a)>16
. We find that either
x_{1}^{+}=x_{1}^{-}
(a contradiction to (
5.36
)), or
x_{2}^{+}=x_{2}^{-}
(a contradiction to (
5.37
)).

Step four
: We now pick a family of
n
mutually orthogonal hyperplanes
\{H_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
such that, denoting by
H_{i}^{\pm}
the corresponding half-spaces, we have

\int_{H_{i}^{\pm}}V(u)=\frac{1}{2}\qquad\forall i=1,...,n\,.

Considering the hyperplanes in pairs and arguing inductively on step three, up to a relabeling we reduce to a situation where there exists a function
v
, symmetric by reflection with respect to each
H_{i}
,
i=1,...,n-1
, and such that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C\,\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v)\,,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}(v)\leq 2^{n}\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(v)\,,\qquad\int_{H_{n}^{\pm}}V(v)=\frac{1}{2}\,.

We can thus consider the functions
v^{\pm}
obtained by reflecting
v
with respect to
H_{n}
as in step three. By (
5.32
) we have

\max\{\delta_{\varepsilon}(v^{+}),\delta_{\varepsilon}(v^{-})\}\leq 2\,\delta_{\varepsilon}(v)\,,\qquad\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C(n)\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{+})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{-})+d_{\Phi}(T_{x^{+}}u_{\varepsilon},T_{x^{-}}u_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,,

where
x^{+}
and
x^{-}
are optimal centers for
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{+};\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}H_{i})
and
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{-};\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}H_{i})
. However,
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}

is a point
, therefore
x^{+}=x^{-}
and we have actually proved

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq C(n)\,\Big\{\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{+})+\alpha_{\varepsilon}(v^{-})\Big\}\,.

Either
v^{+}
or
v^{-}
is an
n
-symmetric function with the required properties.
∎

5.4.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We finally prove Theorem
5.1
. By Theorem
5.5
we can directly assume that
u
is
n
-symmetric. Hence, by Theorem
5.4
, we can directly assume that
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. For
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, the conclusion follows from Theorem
5.3
. Theorem
5.1
is proved.

6.
Proof of the Alexandrov-type theorem

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
, including in particular proof of the Alexandrov-type result of part (iv) of the statement. We begin by proving some of the properties of
\Psi(\sigma,m)
stated in Theorem
1.1
-(i) and not yet discussed. We then review, in section
6.2
, some classical uniqueness and symmetry results for semilinear PDEs in relation to our setting. Finally, in section
6.3
we review how the various results of the paper combines into Theorem
1.1
.

6.1.
Some properties of
\Psi(\sigma,m)

We prove here the properties of
\Psi(\sigma,m)
stated in Theorem
1.1
–(ii). As explained in the introduction, these properties will be crucial in proving Theorem
1.1
-(iv).

Theorem 6.1
.

If
n\geq 2
and
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), then there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}
such that, setting

\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})=\big\{(\sigma,m):0<\sigma<\varepsilon_{0}\,m^{1/n}\big\}\,,

the following holds:

(i): for every
\sigma>0
,
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
is concave on
(0,\infty)
; it is strictly concave on
(0,\infty)
in
n\geq 3
and on
((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
if
n=2
;

(ii):
\Lambda(\sigma,m)
is continuous on
\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})
and

\Big|m^{1/n}\,\Lambda(\sigma,m)-2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\Big|\leq C\,\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\,,\qquad\forall(\sigma,m)\in\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})\,.

(6.1)

(iii):
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
is differentiable with

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial m}(\sigma,m)=\Lambda(\sigma,m)\qquad\forall(\sigma,m)\in\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})\,.

(6.2)

In particular, for every
\sigma>0

\displaystyle\mbox{$\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)$ is strictly increasing on $((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)$}\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{$\Lambda(\sigma,\cdot)$ is strictly decreasing $((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)$}\,.

(iv): for every
m>0
,
\Psi(\cdot,m)
is increasing on
(0,\varepsilon_{0}\,m^{1/n})
.

Proof.

We recall for convenience the scaling formulas

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\rho_{t}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(u)\,,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(\rho_{t}u)|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{(2/n)-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{t}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,t^{(2/n)-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon\,t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)=\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon\,t^{1/n}}(u)}{t^{(n-1)/n}}\,.

(6.4)

\displaystyle\Psi(\sigma,m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{(n-1)/n}\,\psi\Big(\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\Big)\,,

where
\rho_{t}u(x)=u(t^{1/n}\,x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
, and the divide the argument in a few steps.

Step one
: We prove the concavity of
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
. Given
m_{2}>m_{1}>0
,
t\in(0,1)
,
\sigma>0
, and a minimizing sequence
\{w_{j}\}_{j}
for
\Psi(\sigma,t\,m_{1}+(1-t)\,m_{2})
, we set

\alpha_{1}=\frac{t\,m_{1}+(1-t)\,m_{2}}{m_{1}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{2}=\frac{t\,m_{1}+(1-t)\,m_{2}}{m_{2}}\,,

so that
t/\alpha_{1}+(1-t)/\alpha_{2}=1
. Since
\rho_{\alpha_{1}}w_{j}
and
\rho_{\alpha_{2}}w_{j}
are competitors for
\Psi(\sigma,m_{1})
and
\Psi(\sigma,m_{2})
respectively, by the concavity of
t\mapsto t^{(n-2)/n}
(strict if
n\geq 3
), we see that

\displaystyle t\,\Psi(\sigma,m_{1})+(1-t)\,\Psi(\sigma,m_{2})\leq t\,\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}(\rho_{\alpha_{1}}w_{j})+(1-t)\,\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}(\rho_{\alpha_{2}}w_{j})

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{\alpha_{1}}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sigma\,\alpha_{1}^{2/n}\,|\nabla w_{j}|^{2}+\frac{W(w_{j})}{\sigma}\Big)+\frac{1-t}{\alpha_{2}}\,\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sigma\,\alpha_{2}^{2/n}\,|\nabla w_{j}|^{2}+\frac{W(w_{j})}{\sigma}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}(w_{j})+\Big(t\,\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\Big)^{(n-2)/n}+(1-t)\,\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}}\Big)^{(n-2)/n}-1\Big)\,\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla w_{j}|^{2}

(6.6)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}(w_{j})\,.

(6.7)

Letting
j\to\infty
we deduce the concavity of
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
on
(0,\infty)
(strict, if
n\geq 3
). If
n=2
and
m_{1}\geq(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}
, then by Theorem
2.1
we can replace the minimizing sequence
\{w_{j}\}_{j}
in the above argument with a minimizer
w
of
\Psi(\sigma,t\,m_{1}+(1-t)\,m_{2})
. Since
w
solves the Euler-Lagrange equation (
1.9
), there cannot be a
t\neq 1
such that
\rho_{t}w
solves (
1.9
) with the same
\sigma
and some
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
\rho_{\alpha_{i}}w
cannot be a minimizer of
\Psi(\sigma,m_{i})
, and therefore we have a strict inequality in (
6.5
), and no need to take a limit in (
6.7
) (since
\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}(w)=\Psi(\sigma,t\,m_{1}+(1-t)\,m_{2})
).

Step two
: By Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
4.2
for every
m>0
and
\sigma<\varepsilon_{0}\,m^{1/n}
there exists a unique
u_{\sigma,m}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
such that
u_{\sigma,m}
is a minimizer of
\Psi(\sigma,m)
and every other minimizer of
\Psi(\sigma,m)
is a translation of
u_{\sigma,m}
. Moreover, for some
\Lambda(\sigma,m)>0
such that

-2\,\sigma^{2}\,\Delta u_{\sigma,m}=\sigma\,\Lambda(\sigma,m)\,V^{\prime}(u_{\sigma,m})-W^{\prime}(u_{\sigma,m})\,,\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,.

If
u_{\varepsilon}
denotes as usual the unique minimizer of
\psi(\varepsilon)
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, then by (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) we find

u_{\sigma,m}=\rho_{1/m}\,u_{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\varepsilon=\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\,,

and thus

\Lambda(\sigma,m)=\frac{\lambda(\varepsilon)}{m^{1/n}}\,,\qquad\varepsilon=\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\,.

(6.8)

By combining (
6.8
) with Corollary
4.2
and with (
4.7
) we thus find that
\Lambda
is continuous on
\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})
, with

\Big|\Lambda(\sigma,m)-\frac{2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}}{m^{1/n}}\Big|\leq C\,\frac{\sigma}{m^{2/n}}\,.

(6.9)

Step three
: We prove statement (iii). For
(\sigma,m)\in\mathcal{X}(\varepsilon_{0})
, is we set

a(t)=\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma}((1+t)\,u_{\sigma,m})\,,\qquad m(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V((1+t)\,u_{\sigma,m})

then

m^{\prime}(0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Phi(u_{\sigma,m})^{1/(n-1)}\,\sqrt{W(u_{\sigma,m})}\,u_{\sigma,m}>0

and thus there exist
t_{*}>0
and an open interval
I
of
m
such that
m
is strictly increasing from
(-t_{*},t_{*})
to
I
with
m(0)=m
. From
\Psi(\sigma,m(t))\leq a(t)
for every
|t|<t_{*}
and from that fact that
a
is differentiable on
(-t_{*},t_{*})
we deduce that, if
m
is such that
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
is differentiable at
m
, then

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial m}(\sigma,m)=\frac{a^{\prime}(0)}{m^{\prime}(0)}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2\,\nabla u_{\sigma,m}\cdot\nabla u_{\sigma,m}+W^{\prime}(u_{\sigma,m})\,u_{\sigma,m}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{\prime}(u_{\sigma,m})\,u_{\sigma,m}}=\Lambda(\sigma,m)\,.

Now, by statement (i),
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
is differentiable a.e. on
((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
, as well as absolutely continuous, while
\Lambda(\sigma,\cdot)
is continuous on
((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
: by the fundamental theorem of calculus we thus conclude that
(\partial\Psi/\partial m)(\sigma,\cdot)
exists for every
m>(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}
and agrees with
\Lambda(\sigma,m)
.

Step four
: We prove statement (iv). Recalling that

\Psi(\sigma,m)=m^{(n-1)/n}\,\psi\Big(\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\Big)\,,\qquad\forall\sigma,m>0\,,

(6.10)

we see that, since
\Psi(\sigma,\cdot)
is differentiable on
((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
, then
\psi
is differentiable on
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. Since
\psi
is differentiable on
(0,\varepsilon_{0}
, by (
6.10
) we see that
\Psi(\cdot,m)
is differentiable on
(0,\varepsilon_{0}\,m^{1/n})
for every
m>0
, with

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\sigma}=m^{(n-2)/n}\,\psi^{\prime}\Big(\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\Big)\,.

Statement (iv) will thus follow by proving that
\psi^{\prime}>0
on
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. To derive a useful formula for
\psi
we differentiate (
6.10
) in
m
and use (
6.2
) and
\lambda(\sigma/m^{1/n})=m^{1/n}\Lambda(\sigma,m)
to find that

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n}\,\frac{1}{m^{1/n}}\,\psi\Big(\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\Big)-\frac{1}{n}\,\frac{\sigma}{m^{2/n}}\,\psi^{\prime}\Big(\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\Big)=\frac{\lambda(\sigma/m^{1/n})}{m^{1/n}}\,.

In particular, by (
4.5
),

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=(n-1)\,\psi(\varepsilon)-n\,\lambda(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u_{\varepsilon})\,.

By (
3.8
), if we set
\eta_{\varepsilon}(s)=\eta(s-\tau_{\varepsilon})
and change variables according to
|x|=R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s
we find

\varepsilon\,\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\Big\{\Big(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Big)^{2}-W\big(\eta_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon}\big)\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,.

(6.11)

Multiplying by
u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and then integrating on
(r,\infty)
the Euler-Lagrange equation

-2\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\Big\{u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}+(n-1)\,\frac{u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}{r}\Big\}=\varepsilon\,\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})-W^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\,,

we obtain as usual

\varepsilon^{2}\,(u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}-2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\int_{r}^{\infty}\,\frac{(u_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}}{\rho}\,d\rho=W(u_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\,\lambda(\varepsilon)\,V(u_{\varepsilon})\,,

for every
r>0
; by the change of variables
r=R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s
we thus find

(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}-2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}\,dt=W(\eta_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,\varepsilon\,V(\eta_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})

for every
s\in(-R_{0}/\varepsilon,\infty)
. We combine this identity into (
6.11
) to find

\varepsilon\,\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\Big\{2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}\,dt-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,\varepsilon\,V(\eta_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds\,

(6.12)

We now notice that, by (
A.16
), (
A.18
), and (
3.9
) (that is, by the exponential decay of
\eta
,
\eta^{\prime}
,
\eta^{\prime\prime}
and by
|f_{\varepsilon}(s)|\leq C\,\varepsilon\,e^{-|s|/C\,\varepsilon}
for
s>-R_{0}/\varepsilon
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}-(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}\,dt

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\frac{\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\,f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,\frac{\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s)\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s}-2\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\Big(\frac{\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}\Big)^{\prime}\,dt

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-C\,\varepsilon\,e^{-|s|/C}

so that (
6.12
) gives

\varepsilon\,\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\geq\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\Big\{2\,(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,\int_{s}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}\,dt}{R_{0}+\varepsilon\,t}-\lambda(\varepsilon)\,\varepsilon\,V(\eta_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds-C\,\varepsilon^{2}\,.

(6.13)

By (
4.7
), (
3.9
),
R_{0}=\omega_{n}^{-1/n}
and (
6.13
), we have

\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\geq 2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,\int_{-R_{0}/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\,\Big\{\int_{s}^{\infty}\,(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}\,dt-V(\eta_{\varepsilon})\Big\}\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds-C\,\varepsilon\,.

(6.14)

Since
\int_{s}^{\infty}(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{2}=\Phi(\eta_{\varepsilon}(s))
thanks to
\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=-\sqrt{W(\eta_{\varepsilon})}=-\Phi^{\prime}(\eta_{\varepsilon})
, by (
6.14
) we have

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\Phi(\eta_{\varepsilon})-V(\eta_{\varepsilon})\big)\,(R_{0}+\varepsilon\,s)^{n-1}\,ds-C\,\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,R_{0}^{n-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\Phi(\eta)-V(\eta)\big)\,ds-C\,\varepsilon\,.

Since
\Phi
takes values in
(0,1)
,
V=\Phi^{n/(n-1)}<\Phi
on
(0,1)
, and

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\Phi(\eta)-V(\eta)\big)\,ds

is a universal constant. In particular,
\psi^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\geq 1/C
for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
.
∎

6.2.
General criteria for radial symmetry and uniqueness

In this brief section we exploit two classical results from
[
GNN81
]
and
[
PS83
]
to deduce a symmetry and uniqueness result for the kind of semilinear PDE arising as the Euler-Lagrange equation of
\Psi(\sigma,m)
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfy (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), and consider
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma>0
.

(i):
if
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
is a non-zero solution to

-2\,\sigma^{2}\,\Delta u=\sigma\,\ell\,V^{\prime}(u)-W^{\prime}(u)\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,,

(6.15)

with
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
, then
0<u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
.

(ii):
there exists a universal constant
\nu_{0}
such that, if
0<\sigma\,\ell<\nu_{0}
, then, modulo translation, (
6.15
) has a unique solution among functions
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
, with
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
and
0<u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 6.1
.

Notice that the smallness of
\sigma\,\ell
is required only for proving statement (ii).

Proof.

Step one
: We prove statement (i). We intend to apply the following particular case of
[
GNN81
, Theorem 2]
:
if
n\geq 2
,
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
,
u>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
,
-\Delta u+m\,u=g(u)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
m>0
and
g\in C^{1}[0,1]
with
g(t)={\rm O}(t^{1+\alpha})
as
t\to 0^{+}
for some
\alpha>0
, then, up to translations,
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
.

To this end we reformulate (
6.15
) as

-\Delta u+m\,u=g(u)\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,,

(6.16)

where
m=W^{\prime\prime}(0)/2\,\sigma^{2}>0
and

g(t)=\frac{\ell\,V^{\prime}(t)}{2\,\sigma}+\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(0)\,t-W^{\prime}(t)}{2\,\sigma^{2}}\,,\qquad t\in[0,1]\,.

As noticed in section
A.3
,
V\in C^{2,\gamma}[0,1]
for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
, while
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
: in particular
g\in C^{1}[0,1]
. By
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
with
W^{\prime}(0)=0
we have
|W^{\prime}(t)-W^{\prime\prime}(0)\,t||\leq C\,t^{2}
, while (
A.11
) states that
|V^{\prime}(t)|\leq C\,t^{1+\alpha}
for
t\in[0,1]
for some
\alpha>0
, so that

|g(t)|\leq C(n,W,\ell,\sigma)\,t^{1+\alpha}\,,\qquad\forall t\in[0,1]\,.

(6.17)

To check that
u>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we notice that, by (
6.17
), for every
m^{\prime}\in(0,m)
, we can find
t_{0}>0
such that (
6.16
) implies that
-\Delta u+m^{\prime}\,u\geq 0
on the open set
\{u<t_{0}\}
. Since
u\geq 0
and
u
is non-zero, we conclude by the strong maximum principle that
u>0
on
\{u<t_{0}\}
, and thus, on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We are thus in the position to apply the stated particular case of
[
GNN81
, Theorem 2]
and conclude that
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
.

We prove that
u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let us set

f(t)=\frac{\ell\,V^{\prime}(t)}{2\,\sigma}-\frac{W^{\prime}(t)}{2\,\sigma^{2}}\,,\qquad t\in[0,1]\,,

(6.18)

and notice that (
6.15
) is equivalent to
-\Delta u=f(u)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
f
is a Lipschitz function on
[0,1]
with
f(1)=0
, we can find
c>0
such that
f(t)+c\,t
is increasing on
[0,1]
, and rewrite
-\Delta u=f(u)
as

-\Delta\,(1-u)+c\,(1-u)=(f(t)+c\,t)\Big|^{t=1}_{t=u}\geq 0\,.

We thus conclude that
v=1-u
is non-negative on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and such that
-\Delta\,v+c\,v\geq 0
. Since
v
is non-zero (thanks to
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
), by the strong maximum principle we conclude that
v>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, i.e.
u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Step two
: We prove statement (ii). We intend to use
[
PS83
, Theorem 2]
:
if

(a)

f
locally Lipschitz on
(0,\infty)
;

(b)

f(t)/t\to-m
as
t\to 0^{+}
where
m>0
;

(c)

setting
F(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,ds
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
F(\delta)>0
;

(d)

setting
\beta=\inf\{t>0:F(t)>0\}
(so that by (b) and (c),
\beta\in(0,\delta)
), the function
t\mapsto f(t)/(t-\beta)
is decreasing on
(\beta,\infty)\cap\{f>0\}
;

then there is at most one
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{R}_{0}
, with
u>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
, solving
-\Delta u=f(u)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Since, by statement (i), solutions to (
6.15
) satisfy
0<u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, in checking that
f
as in (
6.18
) satisfies the above assumptions it is only the behavior of
f
on
(0,1)
(and not on
(0,\infty)
) that matters. Evidently (a) holds, since
f\in C^{1,\alpha}[0,1]
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Assumption (b) holds with
m=W^{\prime\prime}(0)/2\,\sigma^{2}
. Property (c) holds (with
\delta\in(0,1)
) since

F(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\,f(s)\,ds=\frac{\ell\,V(t)}{2\,\sigma}-\frac{W(t)}{2\,\sigma^{2}}\,,\qquad t\in[0,1]\,,

and
F(1)=(\ell\,V(1)/2\,\sigma)=\ell/2\,\sigma>0
by
\ell>0
and
W(1)=0
. We finally prove (d). Notice that, clearly,
\beta\in(0,1)
and, by continuity of
F
,
F(\beta)=0
, so that, taking (
A.3
) and (
A.6
) into account, and using
\sigma\,\ell<\nu_{0}
and
V(1)=1
,

\frac{\min\{\beta^{2},(1-\beta)^{2}\}}{C}\leq W(\beta)=\sigma\,\ell\,V(\beta)\leq\nu_{0}\,.

(6.19)

If
\nu_{0}<1
, then by (
A.6
) and (
A.11
) we find

2\,\sigma^{2}\,F(t)=\sigma\,\ell\,V(t)-W(t)\leq V(t)-W(t)\leq C\,t^{2n/(n-1)}-\frac{t^{2}}{C}<0\,,\qquad\forall t\in(0,\delta_{0})\,.

(6.20)

By (
6.20
) it must be
\beta\geq\delta_{0}
. Hence, by (
6.19
), if
\nu_{0}
is sufficiently small, then
(1-\beta)^{2}\leq C\,\nu_{0}
. Up to further decrease the value of
\nu_{0}
, we can finally entail that
(\beta,1)\subset(1-\delta_{0},1)
, with
\delta_{0}
as in section
A.3
.

We are now going to check property (d) by showing that

f^{\prime}(t)\,(t-\beta)\leq f(t)\qquad\forall t\in(\beta,1)\,,

(6.21)

(recall that
0<u<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so we can use a version of
[
PS83
, Theorem 2]
localized to
(0,1)
). Using the explicit formula for
f
, (
6.21
) is equivalent to

\sigma\,\ell\,V^{\prime\prime}(t)\,(t-\beta)\leq\sigma\,\ell\,V^{\prime}(t)-W^{\prime}(t)+W^{\prime\prime}(t)\,(t-\beta)\,,\qquad\forall t\in(\beta,1)\,.

(6.22)

By (
A.6
), we have
W^{\prime\prime}(t)(t-\beta)>0
on
(\beta,1)\subset(1-\delta_{0},1)
, and since
V^{\prime}\geq 0
on
[0,1]
, (
6.22
) is implied by checking that, for every
t\in(\beta,1)
,

\displaystyle-W^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma\,\ell\,V^{\prime\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma\,\ell\,\Big\{\frac{n}{(n-1)^{2}}\,\frac{W(t)}{\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{W}\Big)^{(n-2)/(n-1)}}+\frac{n}{n-1}\,\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{W}\Big)^{1/(n-1)}\,\frac{W^{\prime}(t)}{2\,\sqrt{W(t)}}\Big\}\,.

In turn, since
W^{\prime}<0
on
(1-\delta_{0},1)
and
\sigma\,\ell<\nu_{0}<1
, it is actually enough to check that

\displaystyle-W^{\prime}(t)\geq\frac{n}{(n-1)^{2}}\,\frac{W(t)}{\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{W}\Big)^{(n-2)/(n-1)}}\,,\qquad\forall t\in(1-\delta_{0},1)\,.

(6.23)

But, up to further decreasing the value of
\delta_{0}
, this is obvious: indeed (
A.6
) gives
-W^{\prime}(t)\geq(1-t)/C
and
W(t)\leq C\,(1-t)^{2}
for every
t\in(1-\delta_{0},1)
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Theorem
2.1
, Corollary
4.2
, Theorem
6.1
and a scaling argument show the validity of statements (i) and (ii), while statement (iii) follows similarly by scaling and by Theorem
5.1
. To prove the Alexandrov-type theorem, that is, statement (iv)
5
5

5

Notice that we are using
\ell
in (
6.24
) rather than
\lambda
(as done in (
1.22
)) to denote the Lagrange multiplier of
u
. This is meant to avoid confusion with the function
\lambda(\varepsilon)=(\partial\Psi/\partial m)(\varepsilon,1)
appearing in the argument.
we consider
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,1])
, with
u(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
, and solving

-2\,\sigma^{2}\,\Delta u=\sigma\,\ell\,V^{\prime}(u)-W^{\prime}(u)\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}\,,

(6.24)

for some
\sigma
and
\ell
with
0<\sigma\,\ell<\nu_{0}
. By Theorem
6.2
–(i),
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
, and by Theorem
6.2
, provided
\nu_{0}
is small enough, we know that there is at most one radial solution to (
6.24
). Since we know that
u_{\sigma,m}
is a radial solution of (
6.24
) with
\ell=\Lambda(\sigma,m)
, we are left to prove that for every
\ell\in(0,\nu_{0}/\sigma)
there exists a unique
m\in((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
such that
\Lambda(\sigma,m)=\ell
.

To this end, we first notice that, by (
4.7
) and by scaling, for every
\sigma>0
we have

\Lambda(\sigma,m)=\frac{1}{m^{1/n}}\,\lambda\big(\sigma/m^{1/n})\to 0^{+}\qquad\mbox{as $m\to+\infty$}\,.

In particular, since, by Theorem
6.1
,
\Lambda(\sigma,\cdot)
is continuous and strictly decreasing on
((\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n},\infty)
, we have that

\Big\{\Lambda(\sigma,m):m>\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\varepsilon_{0}}\Big)^{n}\Big\}=\Big(0,\Lambda\big(\sigma,(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}\big)\Big)\,.

Now, setting
m=(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}
in (
6.1
), that is, in

\Big|m^{1/n}\,\Lambda(\sigma,m)-2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\Big|\leq C\,\frac{\sigma}{m^{1/n}}\,,

we find that

\Big|\sigma\,\Lambda\big(\sigma,(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}\big)-2\,(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,\varepsilon_{0}\Big|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,,

which implies

\Lambda\big(\sigma,(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}\big)\geq\frac{(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,\varepsilon_{0}}{\sigma}\,,\qquad\forall\sigma>0\,,

provided
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough. Up to further decrease the value of
\nu_{0}
so to have
\nu_{0}\leq(n-1)\,\omega_{n}^{1/n}\,\varepsilon_{0}
, we have proved that

(0,\nu_{0}/\sigma)\subset\Big\{\Lambda(\sigma,m):m>\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\varepsilon_{0}}\Big)^{n}\Big\}\,,

and that for each
\ell\in(0,\nu_{0}/\sigma)
there is a unique
m>(\sigma/\varepsilon_{0})^{n}
such that
\ell=\Lambda(\sigma,m)
, as claimed. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Appendix A
Frequently used auxiliary facts

A.1.
Scaling identities

If
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};[0,\infty))
,
t>0
, we set

\rho_{t}\,u(x)=u(t^{1/n}\,x)\,,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,,

and notice that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\rho_{t}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(u)\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(\rho_{t}u)|^{2}=t^{(2/n)-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{t}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,t^{(2/n)-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon\,t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W(u)=\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon\,t^{1/n}}(u)}{t^{(n-1)/n}}\,.

(A.2)

whenever
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous.

A.2.
Concentration-compactness principle

Denoting by
B_{r}(x)
the ball of center
x
and radius
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and setting
B_{r}=B_{r}(0)
when
x=0
, we provide a reference statement for Lions’ concentration-compactness criterion, which is repeatedly used in our arguments:
if
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j}
is a sequence of probability measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then, up to extract subsequences and compose each
\mu_{j}
with a translation, one the following mutually excluding possibilities holds:

Compactness case:
for every
\tau>0
there exists
R>0
such that

\inf_{j}\,\mu_{j}(B_{R})\geq 1-\tau\,;

Vanishing case:
for every
R>0
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu_{j}(B_{R}(x))=0\,;

Dichotomy case:
there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that for every
\tau>0
one can find
S>0
with
S_{j}\to\infty
such that

\sup_{j}\,|\alpha-\mu_{j}(B_{S})|<\tau\,,\qquad\sup_{j}\,\big|(1-\alpha)-\mu_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{S_{j}})\big|<\tau\,.

Notice that the formulation of the dichotomy case used here is a bit more descriptive than the original one presented in
[
Lio84
, Lemma I]
. Its validity is inferred by a quick inspection of the proof presented in the cited reference.

A.3.
Estimates for
W
,
\Phi
and
V

Throughout the paper we work with a double well potential
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
satisfying (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), that is,

\displaystyle\mbox{$W(0)=W(1)=0$}\,,\quad\mbox{$W>0$ on $(0,1)$}\,,\quad\mbox{$W^{\prime\prime}(0),W^{\prime\prime}(1)>0$}\,,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{W}=1\,.

(A.4)

Frequently used properties of
W
are the validity, for a universal constant
C
, of the expansion

\Big|W(b)-W(a)-W^{\prime}(a)(b-a)-W^{\prime\prime}(a)\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,|b-a|^{3}\,,\qquad\forall a,b\in[0,1]\,,

(A.5)

and the existence of a universal constant
\delta_{0}<1/2
such that

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{W}{t^{2}}\,,\frac{W^{\prime}}{t}\,,W^{\prime\prime}\leq C\quad\hskip 42.67912pt\mbox{on $(0,\delta_{0}]$}\,,\\
&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{W}{(1-t)^{2}}\,,\frac{-W^{\prime}}{1-t}\,,W^{\prime\prime}\leq C\quad\hskip 11.38092pt\mbox{on $[1-\delta_{0},1)$}\,.\end{split}

(A.6)

We can use (
A.6
) to quantify the behaviors near the wells of
\Phi
and, crucially, of
V
. We first notice that, by (
A.3
),
\Phi\in C^{3}_{{\rm loc}}(0,1)
, with

\Phi^{\prime}=\sqrt{W}\,,\quad\Phi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{W^{\prime}}{2\,\sqrt{W}}\,,\quad\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\frac{W^{\prime\prime}}{2\,\sqrt{W}}-\frac{(W^{\prime})^{2}}{4\,W^{3/2}}\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(0,1)$}\,.

By (
A.6
) and (
A.4
) we thus see that
\Phi
satisfies

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{\Phi}{t^{2}}\,,\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{t}\,,\Phi^{\prime\prime}\leq C\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(0,\delta_{0}]$}\,,\\
&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{1-\Phi}{(1-t)^{2}}\,,\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{1-t}\,,-\Phi^{\prime\prime}\leq C\,,\qquad\mbox{on $[1-\delta_{0},1)$}\,,\end{split}

(A.7)

from which we easily deduce

|\Phi(b)-\Phi(a)|\geq\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{C}\,,\qquad\forall a,b\in[0,1]\,.

(A.8)

Moreover, by exploiting (
A.7
) and setting for brevity
a=W^{\prime\prime}(0)
, we see that as
t\to 0^{+}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\,W^{\prime\prime}\,W-(W^{\prime})^{2}}{4\,W^{3/2}}=\frac{2\,(a+{\rm O}(t))\,(a\,(t^{2}/2)+{\rm O}(t^{3}))-(a\,t+{\rm O}(t^{2}))^{2}}{4\,(a\,(t^{2}/2)+{\rm O}(t^{3}))^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm O}(t^{3})}{4\,a^{3/2}\,t^{3}+{\rm o}(t^{3})}\,,

and a similar computation holds for
t\to 1^{-}
, so that

|\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq C\,,\qquad\mbox{on $(0,\delta_{0})\cup(1-\delta_{0},1)$}\,.

(A.9)

By (
A.7
) and (
A.9
) we see that
\Phi\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
with a universal estimate on its
C^{2,1}[0,1]
-norm: in particular,

\Big|\Phi(b)-\Phi(a)-\Phi^{\prime}(a)(b-a)-\Phi^{\prime\prime}(a)\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,|b-a|^{3}\,,\qquad\forall a,b\in(0,1)\,.

(A.10)

Since
V=\Phi^{1+\alpha}
for
\alpha=1/(n-1)\in(0,1]
(recall that
n\geq 2
) and
\Phi(t)=0
if and only if
t=0
, we easily see that
V\in C^{3}_{{\rm loc}}(0,1)
, with

\displaystyle V^{\prime}=(1+\alpha)\,\Phi^{\alpha}\,\Phi^{\prime}\,,\qquad V^{\prime\prime}=(1+\alpha)\Big\{\alpha\,\frac{(\Phi^{\prime})^{2}}{\Phi^{1-\alpha}}+\Phi^{\alpha}\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle|V^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq C(\alpha)\,\Big\{\frac{(\Phi^{\prime})^{3}}{\Phi^{2-\alpha}}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime}\,|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|}{\Phi^{1-\alpha}}+\Phi^{\alpha}\,|\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}|\Big\}\,.

By (
A.10
), and keeping track of the sign of
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
and of the fact that negative powers of
\Phi(t)
are large only near
t=0
, but are bounded near
t=1
, we find that

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{V}{t^{2+2\alpha}}\,,\frac{V^{\prime}}{t^{1+2\alpha}}\,,\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{t^{2\alpha}}\leq C\,,\hskip 19.91684pt\quad|V^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{C}{t^{1-2\alpha}}\qquad\hskip 5.69046pt\mbox{on $(0,\delta_{0}]$}\,,\\
&\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{1-V}{(1-t)^{2}}\,,\frac{V^{\prime}}{1-t}\leq C\,,\quad|V^{\prime\prime}|\,,|V^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq C\,,\qquad\mbox{on $[1-\delta_{0},1)$}\,.\end{split}

(A.11)

In particular,
V\in C^{2,\gamma(n)}[0,1]
,
\gamma(n)=\min\{1,2/(n-1)\}\in(0,1]
, with second order Taylor expansions of the form

\Big|V(b)-V(a)-V^{\prime}(a)(b-a)-V^{\prime\prime}(a)\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{2}\Big|\leq C\,|b-a|^{2+\gamma(n)}\,,\qquad\forall a,b\in(0,1)\,.

(A.12)

We finally notice that we can find a universal constant
C
such that

\frac{t^{2}}{C}\leq W(t)\,,\qquad V(t)\leq C\,t^{2}\,,\qquad V(t)\leq C\,W(t)\,,\qquad\forall t\in(0,1-\delta_{0})\,,

(A.13)

(as it is easily deduced from the bounds on
W
and
V
in (
A.6
) and (
A.11
) and from the fact that
W>0
on
(0,1)
); and that we can also find
C
so that

V(t)\geq\frac{1}{C}\,,\qquad\forall t\in(\delta_{0},1)\,.

(A.14)

A.4.
Estimates for the optimal transition profile
\eta

A crucial object in the analysis of the Allen-Cahn energy is of course the optimal transition profile
\eta
, defined by the first order ODE

\left\{\begin{split}&\eta^{\prime}=-\sqrt{W(\eta)}\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}$}\,,\\
&\eta(0)=\frac{1}{2}\,,\end{split}\right.

(A.15)

which can be seen to satisfy (see, e.g.
[
LM16a
]
)
\eta\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})
,
\eta^{\prime}<0
on
\mathbb{R}
(and
-C\leq\eta^{\prime}\leq-1/C
for
|s|\leq 1
),
\eta(-\infty)=1
, and
\eta(+\infty)=0
, with the exponential decay properties

1-\eta(s)\leq C\,e^{s/C}\qquad\forall s<0\,,\qquad\eta(s)\leq C\,e^{-s/C}\qquad\forall s>0\,,

(A.16)

for a universal constant
C
. Similarly, by combining (
A.16
) with (
A.15
), with the second order ODE satisfied by
\eta
, namely,

2\,\eta^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime}(\eta)\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}$}\,,

(A.17)

and with (
A.6
) we see that also the first and second derivatives of
\eta
decay exponentially

|\eta^{\prime}(s)|\,,|\eta^{\prime\prime}(s)|\leq C\,e^{-|s|/C}\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(A.18)

Combining again (
A.16
) and (
A.6
) we also see that

s\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\,1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau))

belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, with

\tau\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau))\Big)\,ds

increasing in
\tau
and converging to
\mp\infty
as
\tau\to\pm\infty
. In particular, there is a unique universal constant
\tau_{0}
such that

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big)\,ds=0\,.

(A.19)

The constant
\tau_{0}
appears in the computation of the first order expansion of
\psi(\varepsilon)
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
and can be characterized, equivalently, to be

\tau_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{\prime}\,V^{\prime}(\eta)\,s\,ds\,.

(A.20)

Indeed, (
A.19
) gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(1_{(-\infty,0)}(s)-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big)\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Big(1-V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\Big)\,ds-\int_{0}^{\infty}V(\eta(s-\tau_{0}))\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\,ds\,\int_{-\infty}^{s-\tau_{0}}\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{s-\tau_{0}}^{\infty}\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,dt\,.

Both integrands are non-negative, therefore by Fubini’s theorem

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{-\tau_{0}}\,dt\,\int_{t+\tau_{0}}^{0}\,\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,ds-\int_{-\tau_{0}}^{\infty}dt\int_{0}^{t+\tau_{0}}\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-\tau_{0}}\,(t+\tau_{0})\,\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,dt+\int_{-\tau_{0}}^{\infty}(t+\tau_{0})\eta^{\prime}(t)\,V^{\prime}(\eta(t))\,dt

that is

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\eta^{\prime}\,V^{\prime}(\eta)\,t\,dt=-\tau_{0}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{\prime}V^{\prime}(\eta)=V(1)\,\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}\,,

as claimed.
